\gr^{n}\textup{{conj}}\coloneqq Rf_{*}{\mathcal{H}}^{n}({}^{\bullet}_{X/S})[-n]\quad\textup{resp.}\ \gr^{n}\textup{{HDG}}\coloneqq Rf_{*}{}^{n}_{X/S}[-n]

(see Definition
3.6
below).

The Cartier isomorphism gives an equivalence
Rf_{*}{\mathcal{H}}^{n}({}^{\bullet}_{X/S})\simeq Rf^{(1)}_{*}{}^{n}_{X^{(1)}/S}
.
Again by
[
Sta24
, 0FM0]
,
Rf_{*}{}^{n}_{X/S}
commutes with arbitrary base change, and therefore

(\gr^{n}\textup{{HDG}})^{(1)}\simeq(Rf_{*}{}^{n}_{X/S})^{(1)}[-n]\simeq Rf^{(1)}_{*}{}^{n}_{X^{(1)}/S}[-n]\simeq Rf_{*}{\mathcal{H}}^{n}({}^{\bullet}_{X/S})[-n]\simeq\gr^{n}\textup{{conj}}.

We claim that
conj
and
HDG
take values in perfect complexes of
{\mathcal{O}}_{S}
-modules and their respective graded pieces are perfect.

Indeed, first note that we can check this Zariski locally, so we may assume that
S
is affine and in particular quasi-compact. Then for any
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, the complexes
\gr^{n}\textup{{HDG}}
and
Rf_{*}{}^{\bullet}_{X/S}
are perfect, and their formation commutes with arbitrary base change (see
[
Sta24
, 0FM0]
). Since
\sigma_{\geq 0}{}^{\bullet}_{X/S}={}^{\bullet}_{X/S}
, we see inductively using the distinguished triangles above that for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, the complex
\textup{{HDG}}(n)
is perfect. Now certainly the base change of perfect complexes is perfect, and therefore the Cartier isomorphism shows that the graded pieces of
conj
are also perfect. The quasi-compactness of
S
implies that there is an
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
such that
\tau_{\leq n}{}_{X/S}^{\bullet}={}_{X/S}^{\bullet}
, and thus again inductively with the distinguished triangles above, we see that
\textup{{conj}}(n)
is perfect for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}
.

Note that we heavily used that Zariski locally, there is an
n\gg 0
such that for any
k\leq 0
and
j\geq 0
, we have
\textup{{conj}}(k-1)\simeq 0\simeq\textup{{HDG}}(n+j)
and
\textup{{conj}}(n+j)\simeq Rf_{*}{}_{X/S}^{\bullet}\simeq\textup{{HDG}}(k)
, and
\textup{{conj}}(n+j)\rightarrow\textup{{conj}}(n+j+1)
and
\textup{{HDG}}(k+1)\rightarrow\textup{{HDG}}(k)
are equivalent to the identity.

The example above gives us an idea for the definition of
derived
F
-zips
(see Definition
3.15
). Namely, a derived
F
-zip should consist of
two filtrations
(one descending and one ascending) with perfect values that are locally determined by a finite chain of morphisms,
i.e.
functors

C^{\bullet}\in\Fun(\mathbb{Z}^{\op},\textup{Perf}(S))\quad\textup{and}\quad D_{\bullet}\in\Fun(\mathbb{Z},\textup{Perf}(S)),

such that their colimits are equivalent and that on affine opens, they are up to equivalence determined by their values on a finite ordered subset of
\mathbb{Z}
, together with
equivalences

\varphi_{\bullet}
of their graded pieces up to Frobenius twist,
i.e.
for
\gr^{n}C\coloneqq\cofib(C^{n+1}\rightarrow C^{n})
and
\gr^{n}D\coloneqq\cofib(D_{n-1}\rightarrow D_{n})
, equivalences of the form

\varphi_{n}\coloneqq(\gr^{n}C)^{(1)}\xrightarrow{\;\vbox to0.0pt{\vss\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sim$}\vskip-2.0pt}\;}\gr^{n}D.

The
\infty
-category of derived
F
-zips should then be defined as the
\infty
-category of such triples
(C^{\bullet},D_{\bullet},\varphi_{\bullet}).

3.1. Filtrations

In the following,
A
will denote an animated ring.

We will now define the notions of a
filtration
and
graded pieces
and look at properties of filtrations. These definitions are highly influenced by the work of Gwilliam–Pavlov
[
GP18
]
and Example
3.1
.

Definition 3.2
.

A morphism
f\colon M\rightarrow N
of
A
-modules is called a
monomorphism
if for all
i\in\mathbb{Z}
, the morphism
\pi_{i}f
is injective. We call
f
a
split monomorphism
if
f
admits a retraction.

Remark 3.3
.

Note that a monomorphism in our sense is not equivalently an
\infty
-categorical monomorphism;
i.e.
if
f
is a monomorphism of
A
-modules in our sense, then the diagonal
f
may not be an equivalence.
(14)
(14)

(14)

Note that in stable
\infty
-categories, pullback diagrams are equivalently pushout diagrams. So if
f\colon M\rightarrow N
is a morphism of
A
-modules such that the diagonal is an equivalence, then
f
is an equivalence.

Further, let us observe that any split monomorphism is automatically a monomorphism but the other way is not necessarily true.
For this let us note that a split monomorphism
f\colon M\rightarrow N
is equivalently a splitting of the fiber sequence
M\xrightarrow{f}N\rightarrow\cofib(f)
;
i.e.
we have an equivalence of cofiber sequences

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cofib(f)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M\oplus\cofib(f)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cofib(f)\hbox to0.0pt{.\hss}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\id}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\id}

In particular, the natural morphism
\mathbb{Z}\xrightarrow{\cdot 2}\mathbb{Z}
is a monomorphism in our sense but is not split (otherwise, the short exact sequence

0\longrightarrow\mathbb{Z}\overset{\cdot 2}{\longrightarrow}\mathbb{Z}\longrightarrow\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}\longrightarrow 0

would be split).

Definition 3.4
.

An
ascending
(
resp. descending
)
filtration of
A
-modules
is an element
F\in\Fun(\mathbb{Z},\MMod_{A})
(resp.
F\in\Fun(\mathbb{Z}^{\op},\MMod_{A})
).

We call an ascending (resp. descending) filtration
F

(1)

right bounded
if there exists an
i\in\mathbb{Z}
such that the natural map
F(k)\rightarrow\mathop{\textup{colim}}_{\mathbb{Z}}F
(resp.
F(k)\rightarrow\mathop{\textup{colim}}_{\mathbb{Z}^{\op}}F
) is an equivalence for all
i\leq k
(resp.
i\geq k
),

(2)

left bounded
if there exists an
i\in\mathbb{Z}
such that the natural map
0\rightarrow F(k)
is an equivalence for all
k\leq i
(resp.
k\geq i
),

(3)

bounded
if it is left and right bounded,

(4)

perfect
if
F
takes values in
\MMod_{A}^{\perf}
,

(5)

strong
if for all
i\leq j
(resp.
j\leq i
), we have that
F(i)\rightarrow F(j)
is a monomorphism.

Remark 3.5
.

The definition of a
strong filtration
seems natural since for a discrete module
M
over a discrete ring
A
, a filtration is usually defined as a filtered chain of submodules

\dots\subseteq M_{i}\subseteq M_{i+1}\subseteq\dots\subseteq M

(for simplicity, we only consider ascending filtrations). But we can show that the Hodge filtration
HDG
of Example
3.1
is strong
if and only if
the Hodge–de Rham spectral sequence is degenerate and the modules of the
E_{1}
-page are finite locally free (see Theorem
3.74
). Since we are particularly interested in the cases where the Hodge–de Rham spectral sequence is non-degenerate, strong filtrations are not used in the definition of derived
F
-zips.

The
\infty
-category of
A
-modules is stable. Thinking of stable
\infty
-categories as analogues of abelian categories, we may think of cofibers as cokernels. This allows for a definition of graded pieces of a filtration, that was used in Example
3.1
.

Definition 3.6
.

Let
F
be an ascending (resp. descending) filtration of
A
-modules. For any
i\in\mathbb{Z}
, we define the
i^{\mathrm{th}}
graded piece of
F
as
\gr^{i}F\coloneqq\cofib(F(i-1)\rightarrow F(i))
(resp.
\gr^{i}F\coloneqq\cofib(F(i+1)\rightarrow F(i))
).

Remark 3.7
.

By the construction of the category
\Fun(\mathbb{Z},\MMod_{A})
, one sees that two filtrations
F
and
G
are equivalent if and only if there is a morphism
F\rightarrow G
such that for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, the induced morphism
F(n)\rightarrow G(n)
is an equivalence of
A
-modules. However, one can show that a morphism of bounded filtrations is an equivalence if and only if it induces an equivalence on the graded pieces (this is an easy consequence using induction or
[
GP18
, Remark 3.21]
).

Remark 3.8
.

Note that for a perfect filtration
F
of
A
-modules, the graded pieces
\gr^{i}F
are again perfect (since the
\infty
-category of perfect modules is per definition stable; see
[
Lur17
, Section 7.2.4]
).

Remark 3.9
.

We want to attach a monoidal structure to filtrations of
A
-modules (we will only consider ascending filtrations, but the arguments work analogously for descending filtrations as explained at the end of this remark).
First, note that for any (symmetric) monoidal
\infty
-category
{\mathcal{C}}
, the
\infty
-category
\Fun(\mathbb{Z},{\mathcal{C}})
has two monoidal structures. The first one is simply given by termwise tensor product (see
[
Lur17
, Remark 2.1.3.4]
); the other one is given by the Day convolution (see
[
Lur17
, Example 2.2.6.17]
).
We will not use the monoidal structure given by termwise tensor product since we want to consider bounded filtrations, and for such we do not have a unit element with respect to the termwise tensor product. Having this in mind, we will look closely into the monoidal structure induced by the Day convolution, which we will explain in the following.

For the Day convolution, we first need a (symmetric) monoidal structure on
\mathbb{Z}
. For this, we simply take
\mathbb{Z}
with the usual addition, seen as a symmetric monoidal structure on
\mathbb{Z}
. Then the Day convolution of two elements
F,G\in\Fun(\mathbb{Z},\MMod_{A})
, denoted by
F\otimes G
, is given by the formula

(F\otimes G)(k)\simeq\mathop{\textup{colim}}_{n+m\leq k}F(n)\otimes_{A}G(m),

where we take the colimit over the category of triples
(a,b,a+b\rightarrow k)
where
a,b\in\mathbb{Z}
and
a+b\rightarrow k
is a morphism in
\mathbb{Z}
(recall that this simply means
a+b\leq k
) and the morphisms are given componentwise,
i.e.
a morphism

(a,b,a+b\rightarrow k)\longrightarrow(a^{\prime},b^{\prime},a^{\prime}+b^{\prime}\rightarrow k^{\prime})

is given by the relations
a\leq a^{\prime}
,
b\leq b^{\prime}
and
k\leq k^{\prime}
. A unit element for this tensor product is given by the bounded perfect filtration
A_{\bullet}^{\textup{triv}}
on
A
, where
A_{i}^{\textup{triv}}\simeq A
for
i\geq 0
and
A_{i}^{\textup{triv}}=0
otherwise, the maps
A_{m}^{\textup{triv}}\rightarrow A_{n}^{\textup{triv}}
for
0\leq m\leq n
are given by the identity and the maps
A_{m}^{\textup{triv}}\rightarrow A_{n}^{\textup{triv}}
for
m\leq n\leq 0
are given by
0
.

If we replace ascending filtrations with descending ones, the relations above get opposed;
i.e.
we have unique morphisms
a\rightarrow b
in
\mathbb{Z}^{\op}
if and only if
b\leq a
. Taking this to account, we can dually define the Day convolution for descending filtrations similarly.

Notation 3.10
.

In the definition of derived
F
-zips, we will have an ascending and a descending filtration. For clarity, for an ascending filtration
F\in\Fun(\mathbb{Z},\MMod_{A})
, we denote its values by
F_{n}\coloneqq F(n)
for any
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, and for a descending filtration
G\in\Fun(\mathbb{Z}^{\op},\MMod_{A})
, we denote its values by
G^{n}\coloneqq G(n)
. We also denote the filtrations by
F_{\bullet}\coloneqq F
and
G^{\bullet}\coloneqq G
.
For the gradings, we omit the
\bullet
;
i.e.
we write
\gr^{i}F\coloneqq\gr^{i}F_{\bullet}
and
\gr^{i}G\coloneqq\gr^{i}G^{\bullet}

Remark 3.11
.

Let us visualize the Day convolution using an easy example. Let
M
and
N
be
A
-modules, and let
C\rightarrow M
and
D\rightarrow N
be morphisms of
A
-modules. Now let us look at the filtrations
C_{\bullet}
and
D_{\bullet}
given

\displaystyle C_{\bullet}

\displaystyle\colon\cdots\xrightarrow{\;\;0\;\;}0\xrightarrow{\;\;0\;\;}C\longrightarrow M\xrightarrow{\;\id\;}M\xrightarrow{\;\id\;}\cdots

\displaystyle D_{\bullet}

\displaystyle\colon\cdots\xrightarrow{\;\;0\;\;}0\xrightarrow{\;\;0\;\;}D\longrightarrow N\xrightarrow{\;\id\;}N\xrightarrow{\;\id\;}\cdots\hbox to0.0pt{,\hss}

where we set
C_{0}=C
and
D_{0}=D
. Then we have
(C_{\bullet}\otimes D_{\bullet})_{0}\simeq C\otimes_{A}D
, and the
A
-module
(C_{\bullet}\otimes D_{\bullet})_{1}
is given by the pushout of the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C\otimes_{A}D}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M\otimes_{A}D}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C\otimes_{A}N\hbox to0.0pt{.\hss}}

The
A
-module
(C_{\bullet}\otimes D_{\bullet})_{2}
is given by the colimit of the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C\otimes_{A}D}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M\otimes_{A}D}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C\otimes_{A}N}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M\otimes_{A}D}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M\otimes_{A}N}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C\otimes_{A}N\hbox to0.0pt{;\hss}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\id}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\id}

in particular, we may forget about the topmost module and only look at the colimit of the bottom zigzag (in the homotopy category). This diagram makes clear that
(C_{\bullet}\otimes D_{\bullet})_{2}\simeq M\otimes_{A}N
. The same visualization works for higher degrees of the filtration
(C_{\bullet}\otimes D_{\bullet})_{\bullet}
, and we will prove in the following proposition that the Day convolution descends to perfect bounded filtrations having this tree structure in mind.

Proposition 3.12
.

The Day convolution on
\Fun(\mathbb{Z},\MMod_{A})
descends to a symmetric monoidal structure on the full subcategory of perfect bounded ascending filtrations.
The same holds for perfect bounded descending filtrations.

Proof.

That the unit element for the Day convolution is a bounded perfect filtration on an
A
-module is shown in Remark
3.9
.

Let
C_{\bullet}
and
D_{\bullet}
be bounded ascending filtrations of
A
-modules. We claim that
(C_{\bullet}\otimes D_{\bullet})_{\bullet}
is a bounded ascending filtration.
That
(C_{\bullet}\otimes D_{\bullet})_{\bullet}
defines a left-bounded filtration is clear. To see that it is also right bounded, fix some integers
k,k^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}
such that the natural morphisms

C_{i}\xrightarrow{\;\vbox to0.0pt{\vss\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sim$}\vskip-2.0pt}\;}\mathop{\textup{colim}}_{\mathbb{Z}}C_{\bullet}\quad\textup{and}\quad D_{j}\xrightarrow{\;\vbox to0.0pt{\vss\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sim$}\vskip-2.0pt}\;}\mathop{\textup{colim}}_{\mathbb{Z}}D_{\bullet}

are equivalences for all
i\geq k
and
j\geq k^{\prime}
. For simplicity, let us set
M\coloneqq\mathop{\textup{colim}}_{\mathbb{Z}}C_{\bullet}
and
N\coloneqq\mathop{\textup{colim}}_{\mathbb{Z}}D_{\bullet}
. First, note that the morphism
C_{i\leq i+1}\colon C_{i}\rightarrow C_{i+1}
is an equivalence for all
i\geq k
. Using this equivalence, we may assume that
C_{i\leq i+1}
is given by
\id_{M}
(it is not hard to find an equivalence of filtrations); we do the same for
D_{\bullet}
. Now let us look at
(C_{\bullet}\otimes D_{\bullet})_{k+k^{\prime}}
; we claim that this term is equivalent to
M\otimes N
. Indeed,
C_{k}\otimes_{A}D_{k^{\prime}}\simeq M\otimes_{A}N
by construction. Now let
(i,j)\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
be such that
i+j\leq k+k^{\prime}
but
i>k
or
j>k^{\prime}
, so there is no morphism from
C_{i}\otimes_{A}D_{j}
to
C_{k}\otimes_{A}D_{k^{\prime}}
. Without loss of generality, assume
i>k
(in particular,
j<k^{\prime}
).

Let us visualize what we are going to do. Considering the zigzag from Remark
3.11
, we will look at the following diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{k}\otimes_{A}D_{j}}

.

.

.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{k+1}\otimes_{A}D_{j}}

.

.

.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{k}\otimes_{A}D_{k^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{i}\otimes_{A}D_{j}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(C_{\bullet}\otimes D_{\bullet})_{k+k^{\prime}}\hbox to0.0pt{.\hss}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\id}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\id}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\id}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}

By the definition of colimits, we automatically get a homotopy between
f
and
h
and a homotopy between
f
and
g
. In particular, this diagram shows that
h
is, up to homotopy, uniquely determined by
f
and
g
. But certainly the morphisms of the filtrations and
g
uniquely (up to homotopy) determine
f
. Using this and the universal property of colimits, we see that there exists a morphism
p\colon(C_{\bullet}\otimes D_{\bullet})_{k+k^{\prime}}\rightarrow C_{k}\otimes_{A}D_{k^{\prime}}
such that
\id_{C_{k}\otimes_{A}D_{k^{\prime}}}\simeq p\circ g
. But
g\circ p
induces a map
(C_{\bullet}\otimes D_{\bullet})_{k+k^{\prime}}\rightarrow(C_{\bullet}\otimes D_{\bullet})_{k+k^{\prime}}
that is compatible with all transition maps in the colimit diagram, and thus
g\circ p\simeq\id_{(C_{\bullet}\otimes D_{\bullet})_{k+k^{\prime}}}
. In other words,
M\otimes_{A}N\simeq C_{k}\otimes_{A}D_{k^{\prime}}\simeq(C_{\bullet}\otimes D_{\bullet})_{k+k^{\prime}}
.

The same argument shows that
(C_{\bullet}\otimes D_{\bullet})_{l}\simeq M\otimes_{A}N
and that the canonical maps
(C_{\bullet}\otimes D_{\bullet})_{l}\rightarrow(C_{\bullet}\otimes D_{\bullet})_{l+1}
are homotopic to the identity for all
l\geq k+k^{\prime}.
So for all
l\geq k+k^{\prime}
, the natural map

(C_{\bullet}\otimes D_{\bullet})_{l}\xrightarrow{\;\vbox to0.0pt{\vss\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sim$}\vskip-2.0pt}\;}\mathop{\textup{colim}}_{\mathbb{Z}}(C_{\bullet}\otimes D_{\bullet})_{\bullet}\simeq M\otimes_{A}N

is an equivalence;
i.e.

(C_{\bullet}\otimes D_{\bullet})_{\bullet}
is right bounded.

The above computations show that since
C_{\bullet}
and
D_{\bullet}
are bounded, for any
k\in\mathbb{Z}
, we have that
(C_{\bullet}\otimes D_{\bullet})_{k}
is equivalent to the colimit taken over a finite filtered subset of
\mathbb{Z}
(again seen as a category via the nerve functor and morphisms uniquely given by relations). Since finite colimits of perfect modules are perfect, we see that
(C_{\bullet}\otimes D_{\bullet})_{\bullet}
is not only bounded but also perfect (note that stable
\infty
-categories are closed under finite colimits; see
[
Lur17
, Proposition 1.1.3.4]
).

Combining everything above, we see that the Day convolution descends to bounded perfect filtrations and therefore gives us a symmetric monoidal structure on bounded perfect filtrations (see
[
Lur17
, Proposition 2.2.1.1, Remark 2.2.1.2]
).

The proof for descending filtrations works analogously.
∎

Remark 3.13
.

For two filtrations
C_{\bullet}
and
D_{\bullet}
of
A
-modules, it is known that

\gr^{k}(C_{\bullet}\otimes D_{\bullet})\simeq\bigoplusop\displaylimits_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\gr^{n}C\otimes_{A}\gr^{k-n}D

(see
[
BMS19
, Lemma 5.2]
).

Remark 3.14
.

Let us note that the construction of the Day convolution can also be done for
\Fun({\mathcal{C}},{\mathcal{D}})
, where
{\mathcal{C}}
and
{\mathcal{D}}
are arbitrary symmetric monoidal
\infty
-categories (see
[
Lur17
, Example 2.2.6.17]
).

An interesting example for us occurs if
{\mathcal{C}}\simeq\mathbb{Z}^{\textup{disc}}
(recall that this means the set
\mathbb{Z}
as a discrete
1
-category and thus an
\infty
-category via the nerve functor), where we endow
\mathbb{Z}^{\disc}
with a symmetric monoidal structure by addition,
i.e.

a\otimes b\coloneqq a+b
, and
{\mathcal{D}}\simeq\MMod_{A}^{\perf}
. Then for functors
F,G\in\Fun(\mathbb{Z}^{\textup{disc}},\MMod_{A}^{\perf})
, we have

(F\otimes G)(k)\simeq\bigoplusop\displaylimits_{n+m=k}F(n)\otimes_{A}G(m)\simeq\bigoplusop\displaylimits_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}F(n)\otimes_{A}G(k-n).

This will become important when constructing a symmetric monoidal structure on derived
F
-zips since we have to take into account the morphisms between graded pieces and the behaviour of graded pieces of the tensor product of bounded perfect filtrations.

3.2. Derived
\boldsymbol{F}
-zips over affine schemes

We are ready to define derived
F
-zips, and we will do so by axiomatizing the structures occurring in Example
3.1
. We will first restrict ourselves to the local case;
i.e.
we define derived
F
-zips over animated rings. The reason, besides simplicity, is that the theory of derived algebraic geometry was only developed for animated rings since we want a “nice” model category such as the model category associated to animated rings. This is not a real issue since globalization of the results is achieved by considering right Kan extensions. There is also a direct way of defining derived
F
-zips for derived stacks, but we will see that the two constructions agree (see Remark
3.65
).

Recall that we fixed an
\mathbb{F}_{p}
-algebra
R
at the beginning of this section.

Definition 3.15
.

Let
A
be an animated
R
-algebra.
A
derived
F
-zip over
A
is a tuple
(C^{\bullet},D_{\bullet},\phi,\varphi_{\bullet})
consisting of

•

a descending bounded perfect filtration of
A
-modules
C^{\bullet}
,

•

an ascending bounded perfect filtration of
A
-modules
D_{\bullet}
,

•

an equivalence
\phi\colon\mathop{\textup{colim}}_{\mathbb{Z}^{\op}}C^{\bullet}\simeq\mathop{\textup{colim}}_{\mathbb{Z}}D_{\bullet},
and

•

a family of equivalences
\varphi_{k}\colon(\gr^{k}C)^{(1)}\xrightarrow{\vbox to0.0pt{\vss\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sim$}\vskip-2.0pt}}\gr^{k}D
.

The
\infty
-category of
F
-zips over
A
, denoted by
\FZip_{\infty,R}(A)
, is defined as the full subcategory of

\displaystyle(\Fun(\mathbb{Z}^{\op},\MMod_{A}^{\textup{perf}})

\displaystyle\times_{\mathop{\textup{colim}},\MMod_{A},\mathop{\textup{colim}}}\Fun(\mathbb{Z},\MMod_{A}^{\textup{perf}}))

\displaystyle\times_{((\gr^{i}_{-})^{(1)},\gr^{i}_{-})_{i\in\mathbb{Z}},\prodop\displaylimits_{\mathbb{Z}}\Fun(\partial\Delta^{1},\MMod_{A}^{\perf})}\prodop\displaylimits{Z}\Fun(\Delta^{1},\MMod_{A}^{\perf})

consisting of derived
F
-zips over
A
.

For an animated
R
-algebra homomorphism
A\rightarrow A^{\prime}
, we have an obvious base change functor
\FZip_{\infty,R}(A)\rightarrow\FZip_{\infty,R}(A^{\prime})
via the tensor product, where the filtrations are base changed componentwise with induced morphisms.

Remark 3.16
.

In the above definition we have to fix the equivalence between the colimits of the ascending and descending filtrations. This comes from the fact that we want to define derived
F
-zips as a full subcategory, as above. To be more specific, let us look at a pullback diagram of
\infty
-categories

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{D}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{A}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{B}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{C}}\hbox to0.0pt{.\hss}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces n}

A morphism
\Delta^{0}\rightarrow{\mathcal{D}}
is by definition the same as a diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta^{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{A}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{B}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{C}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces n}

with equivalences
g\circ f\simeq h
and
n\circ m\simeq h
in the
\infty
-category
\Fun(\Delta^{0},{\mathcal{C}})
,
i.e.

1
-morphisms in
\Hom_{\textup{Cat}_{\infty}}(\Delta^{0},{\mathcal{C}})\coloneqq\Fun(\Delta^{0},{\mathcal{C}})^{\simeq}
(resp.
2
-morphisms in
\textup{Cat}_{\infty}
). Important here is that we have to fix the homotopy equivalences
g\circ f\simeq h
and
n\circ m\simeq h
;
i.e.
they are an additional datum. So an object in
{\mathcal{D}}
is the same as a tuple
(A,B,C)\in{\mathcal{A}}\times{\mathcal{B}}\times{\mathcal{C}}
together with equivalences
g(A)\simeq C
and
n(B)\simeq C
. This is equivalent to giving a tuple
(A,B,\phi)
of objects
A\in{\mathcal{A}}
and
B\in{\mathcal{B}}
and an equivalence
\phi\colon g(A)\simeq n(B)
.

Remark 3.17
.

Let
A
be an animated
R
-algebra. The homotopy category of derived
F
-zips over
A
forgets the extra datum of the equivalence between the colimits. This follows from the fact that the filtrations in the definition of a derived
F
-zips over
A
are bounded. So, any derived
F
-zip
(C^{\bullet},D_{\bullet},\phi,\varphi_{\bullet})
is isomorphic (not canonically) in
h\FZip_{\infty}(S)
to a derived
F
-zip, where the equivalence between the colimits is actually given by the identity. In particular, up to equivalence we may replace
\phi
with the identity, and we will write
(C^{\bullet},D_{\bullet},\varphi_{\bullet})
in this case for a derived
F
-zip when we work with derived
F
-zips up to homotopy.

Example 3.18
.

Let us come back to Example
3.1
. Let
f\colon X\rightarrow\Spec(A)
be a proper smooth morphism of schemes. Then the associated Hodge and conjugate filtrations
HDG
and
conj
define, respectively, descending, and ascending perfect bounded filtrations of
A
-modules. We also have equivalences
\varphi_{n}\colon(\gr^{n}\textup{{HDG}})^{(1)}\xrightarrow{\vbox to0.0pt{\vss\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sim$}\vskip-2.0pt}}\gr^{n}\textup{{conj}}
between the graded pieces (up to Frobenius twist), induced by the Cartier isomorphism. Therefore, we get a derived
F
-zip associated to the proper smooth map
f
of schemes

\underline{R\Gamma_{\dR}(X/A)}\coloneqq(\textup{{HDG}}^{\bullet},\textup{{conj}}_{\bullet},\varphi_{\bullet})

(note that
\mathop{\textup{colim}}_{\mathbb{Z}^{\op}}\textup{{HDG}}^{\bullet}\simeq\mathop{\textup{colim}}_{\mathbb{Z}}\textup{{conj}}
naturally by the identity).

Remark 3.19
.

Let us note that for any
A\in\textup{AR}_{R}
, the
\infty
-category of
F
-zips over
A
is essentially small, even if we do not assume
\MMod_{A}
to be small.
(15)
(15)

(15)

We want to note that we did not assume any smallness of the module categories explicitly, and this remark shows that it is not needed in this section. But as Remark
2.43
shows, we need smallness of the module categories for globalization purposes,
i.e.
when we want to extend derived
F
-zips to derived schemes via right Kan extension.
This is because the
\infty
-category
\MMod_{A}
is compactly generated (see
[
Lur17
, Proposition 7.2.4.2]
) (thus accessible), and therefore the full subcategory of perfect objects is essentially small (see
[
Lur09
, Proposition 5.4.2.2]
). Hence for any small
\infty
-category
K
, the
\infty
-category
\Fun(K,\MMod^{\perf}_{A})
is again essentially small (see
[
Lur09
, Propositions 5.3.4.13 and 5.4.4.3]
).
Finally, since
\FZip_{\infty,R}(A)
is a full subcategory of finite limits of the objects of the form above, we see that indeed
\FZip_{\infty,R}(A)
is essentially small (note that by
[
Lur09
, Corollary 4.2.4.8]
, the
\infty
-category of small
\infty
-categories has small limits).

Lemma 3.20
.

The
\infty
-category of derived
F
-zips over an animated
R
-algebra
A
is stable.

Proof.

We know that
\MMod_{A}
and
\MMod^{\perf}_{A}
are stable and thus also that for any
\infty
-category
{\mathcal{C}}
, the
\infty
-category
\Fun(C,\MMod_{A}^{\perf})
is stable. Since the limit of stable
\infty
-categories with finite limit-preserving transition maps is stable, it is enough to show that
\FZip_{\infty,R}(A)
is a stable subcategory of

\displaystyle\left(\Fun\left(\mathbb{Z}^{\op},\MMod_{A}^{\textup{perf}}\right)\right.

\displaystyle\left.\times_{\mathop{\textup{colim}},\MMod_{A},\mathop{\textup{colim}}}\Fun\left(\mathbb{Z},\MMod_{A}^{\textup{perf}}\right)\right)

\displaystyle\times_{((\gr^{i}_{-})^{(1)},\gr^{i}_{-})_{i\in\mathbb{Z}},\prodop\displaylimits_{\mathbb{Z}}\Fun\left(\partial\Delta^{1},\MMod_{A}^{\perf}\right)}\prodop\displaylimits{Z}\Fun\left(\Delta^{1},\MMod_{A}^{\perf}\right)

(note that
\prodop\displaylimits{Z}\Fun(\Delta^{1},\MMod_{A}^{\perf})\simeq\Fun(\mathbb{Z}^{\disc},\Fun(\Delta^{1},\MMod_{A}^{\perf}))
and filtered colimits preserve finite limits).
For this, we have to show that the perfect bounded filtrations, equivalences between colimits of filtrations and equivalences between the graded pieces (up to Frobenius twist) are stable under shifts and cofibers.

That perfect bounded filtrations are stable under shift and cofibers follows immediately from the fact that limits and colimits of functors can be computed pointwise (see
[
Lur09
, Corollary 5.1.2.3]
). The same argument implies that equivalences between the graded pieces (up to Frobenius twist) are stable under cofibers and shifts. Since filtered colimits commute with shifts and cofibers, we also see that the equivalence between the colimits is preserved under those operations.
∎

In the following, we want to construct a symmetric monoidal structure on derived
F
-zips. The idea is very simple. We know that derived
F
-zips are contained in a larger
\infty
-category (see Definition
3.15
); let us denote this category with
{\mathcal{C}}
. This
\infty
-category
{\mathcal{C}}
is constructed by limits of functor categories, that we can endow with the Day convolution. For the morphisms between graded pieces, we have to be a bit careful, but Remarks
3.13
and
3.14
show us that this will not be a problem. Since passing to the graded pieces and taking the colimit of a filtration are both monoidal functors, we see that indeed
{\mathcal{C}}
is symmetric monoidal. Now we only need to show that the unit object of
{\mathcal{C}}
is a derived
F
-zip, which follows immediately, that the Day convolution of perfect bounded filtrations is bounded perfect (this is Proposition
3.12
) and that the induced morphism of graded pieces (up to Frobenius twist) is an equivalence, which is also immediate.

Proposition 3.21
.

The
\infty
-category of derived
F
-zips over an animated
R
-algebra
A
admits a symmetric monoidal structure.

Proof.

We know that
\MMod_{A}^{\perf}
admits a symmetric monoidal structure (see
[
Lur17
, Remark 2.2.1.2]
and note that as an
A
-module,
A
is perfect and the tensor product of perfect
A
-modules is again perfect). We now show how to construct a symmetric monoidal structure on derived
F
-zips.

The monoidal structure on the filtrations is given by the Day convolution (see Proposition
3.12
). The monoidal structure on the equivalences of graded pieces is given in the following way.

We endow
\Fun(\mathbb{Z}^{\disc},\MMod_{A}^{\perf})
with the Day convolution (as explained in Remark
3.14
), where we endow
\mathbb{Z}^{\disc}
with a symmetric monoidal structure by usual addition. The unit object in
\Fun(\mathbb{Z}^{\disc},\MMod_{A}^{\perf})
is given by
A_{\textup{triv}}^{\disc}
, where
A_{\textup{triv}}^{\disc}(n)\simeq A
if
n=0
and
0
otherwise.
Now we endow the
\infty
-category
\Fun(\Delta^{1},\Fun(\mathbb{Z}^{\disc},\MMod_{A}^{\perf}))
with the pointwise tensor product (see
[
Lur17
, Remark 2.1.3.4]
); we do exactly the same for
\Fun(\partial\Delta^{1},\Fun(\mathbb{Z}^{\disc},\MMod_{A}^{\perf})).
Certainly, by this construction, for a derived
F
-zip
(C^{\bullet},D_{\bullet},\varphi_{\bullet})
over
A
, the family
\varphi_{\bullet}
defines an element of
\Fun(\Delta^{1},\Fun(\mathbb{Z}^{\disc},\MMod_{A}^{\perf}))
.

Now let us note that taking the colimit defines a symmetric monoidal functor from ascending (resp. descending) filtrations to
\MMod_{A}
(as the tensor product of spectra commute with colimits in each variable, see
[
Lur17
, Corollary 4.8.2.19]
). Also, sending a filtration to its graded piece is symmetric monoidal by Remark
3.13
. Therefore, we can attach a symmetric monoidal structure to

\displaystyle{\mathcal{C}}(A)\coloneqq\left(\Fun\left(\mathbb{Z}^{\op},\MMod_{A}^{\textup{perf}}\right)\right.

\displaystyle\times_{\mathop{\textup{colim}},\MMod_{A},\mathop{\textup{colim}}}\Fun\left.\left(\mathbb{Z},\MMod_{A}^{\textup{perf}}\right)\right)

\displaystyle\times_{\Fun\left(\partial\Delta^{1},\Fun\left(\mathbb{Z}^{\disc},\MMod_{A}^{\perf}\right)\right)}\Fun\left(\Delta^{1},\Fun\left(\mathbb{Z}^{\disc},\MMod_{A}^{\perf}\right)\right),

where we use that the
\infty
-category of symmetric monoidal
\infty
-categories has limits (see
(16)
(16)

(16)

The reference shows the existence of limits in commutative algebra objects of symmetric monoidal
\infty
-categories. But using
[
Lur17
, Proposition 4.1.7.10]
, we can endow the
\infty
-category of
\infty
-categories with the Cartesian model structure (a concrete description of the associated
\infty
-operad is given in
[
Lur17
, Notation 4.8.1.2]
). The commutative algebra objects of the
\infty
-category of
\infty
-categories with this monoidal structure is then equivalent to the
\infty
-category of symmetric monoidal
\infty
-categories (defined for example in
[
Lur17
, Variant 2.1.4.13]
).

[
Lur17
, Remark 2.4.2.6, Proposition 3.2.2.1]
).

Since derived
F
-zips over
A
form a full subcategory of
{\mathcal{C}}(A)
, it suffices to check that the unit element of
{\mathcal{C}}(A)
is in
\FZip_{\infty,R}(A)
and that it is closed under the tensor product (see
[
Lur17
, Remark 2.2.1.2]
). But this follows from Proposition
3.12
and Remarks
3.13
and
3.14
. Concretely, the unit element in
\FZip_{\infty,R}(A)
is given by

\underline{\mathbbl{1}_{A}}\coloneqq(A^{\bullet}_{\textup{triv}},A_{\bullet}^{\textup{triv}},\id_{A},(\id_{A})_{0}),

where
A_{\bullet}^{\textup{triv}}
is defined as in Remark
3.9
,
A_{\textup{triv}}^{\bullet}
is defined dually,
i.e.
is given by
A_{\textup{triv}}^{n}\simeq A
for
n\leq 0
and
0
elsewhere and has identity as transition maps,
(\id_{A})_{0}
denotes the family of morphisms
\varphi_{\bullet}
, where
\varphi_{0}\simeq\id_{A}
and
\varphi_{n}=0
elsewhere.

Note that for
\varphi_{\bullet},\vartheta_{\bullet}\in\Fun(\Delta^{1},\Fun(\mathbb{Z}^{\disc},\MMod_{A}^{\perf}))
which induce equivalences in
\Fun(\mathbb{Z}^{\disc},\MMod_{A}^{\perf})
, their tensor product is still an equivalence, by the explicit description given in Remark
3.14
.
∎

Our next goal is to show that the functor that sends an animated
R
-algebra to the
\infty
-category of derived
F
-zips over it is locally geometric. For this we need that it is a hypercomplete sheaf for the étale topology. We will show that it is a hypercomplete sheaf even for the fpqc topology. Since every geometric derived stack is hypercomplete (see Lemma
2.51
), the hypercompleteness condition is necessary, at least for the étale topology.

Again the idea is very simple and follows the proof of descent for perfect modules seen in
[
AG14
, proof of Lemma 5.4]
. We again embed
F
-zips into a larger category as in the proof of Proposition
3.21
, which satisfies hyperdescent. Then we only need to check that the property
bounded perfect
of a filtration and the property
equivalence
of a morphism between modules satisfy fpqc hyperdescent. But since our cover is affine and perfectness is equivalent to dualizability, both properties satisfy hyperdescent, and we are done.

To see that the larger category satisfies descent, one only needs that perfect filtrations satisfy descent, which will follow the from descent of perfect modules.

Lemma 3.22
.

Let
F\colon\textup{AR}_{R}\rightarrow\textup{Cat}_{\infty}
be a hypercomplete fpqc sheaf. Then for any
\infty
-category
{\mathcal{C}}
, the functor
\Fun({\mathcal{C}},F(-))\colon\textup{AR}_{R}\rightarrow\textup{Cat}_{\infty}
is a hypercomplete fpqc sheaf.

Proof.

As
\Fun({\mathcal{C}},-)
is right adjoint to the product, it preserves limits. Since
F
is a hypercomplete sheaf, we see that indeed the natural morphism
\Fun({\mathcal{C}},F(A))\xrightarrow{\vbox to0.0pt{\vss\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sim$}\vskip-2.0pt}}\lim_{\Delta_{s}}\Fun({\mathcal{C}},F(A^{\bullet}))
is an equivalence for any fpqc hypercovering
A\rightarrow A^{\bullet}
.
∎

Definition and Proposition 3.23
.

The functor

\displaystyle\FZip_{\infty,R}\colon\textup{AR}_{R}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\textup{Cat}_{\infty}

\displaystyle A

\displaystyle\longmapsto\FZip_{\infty,R}(A)

is a hypercomplete sheaf for the fpqc topology.

Proof.

In the following, we will denote the functor
\textup{AR}_{R}\rightarrow\textup{Cat}_{\infty},\ A\mapsto\MMod^{\perf}_{A}
by
\MMod^{\perf}_{(-)}
to avoid confusion with the notation of the stack of perfect modules over
R
.

Let
A\rightarrow A^{\bullet}
be an fpqc hypercovering given by a functor
\Delta_{+,s}\rightarrow\textup{AR}_{R}
. We have to show that
\FZip_{\infty}(A)\rightarrow\lim_{\Delta_{s}}\FZip_{\infty,R}(A^{\bullet})
is an equivalence.

For convenience, we first set

\displaystyle{\mathcal{C}}(-)\coloneqq\Fun\left(\mathbb{Z}^{\op},\MMod_{-}^{\textup{perf}}\right)

\displaystyle\times_{\mathop{\textup{colim}},\MMod_{-},\mathop{\textup{colim}}}\Fun\left(\mathbb{Z},\MMod_{-}^{\textup{perf}}\right)

\displaystyle\times_{\Fun\left(\partial\Delta^{1},\Fun\left(\mathbb{Z}^{\disc},\MMod_{-}^{\perf}\right)\right)}\Fun\left(\Delta^{1},\Fun\left(\mathbb{Z}^{\disc},\MMod_{-}^{\perf}\right)\right).

For the fully faithfulness, let us look at the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\FZip_{\infty,R}(A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lim_{\Delta_{s}}\FZip_{\infty,R}(A^{\bullet})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{C}}(A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lim_{\Delta_{s}}{\mathcal{C}}(A^{\bullet})\hbox to0.0pt{.\hss}}

By Lemma
3.22
and descent of (perfect) modules (see Remark
2.43
), we see that
{\mathcal{C}}
is a hypercomplete sheaf for the fpqc topology, and thus the bottom horizontal arrow is an equivalence, and thus the upper horizontal arrow is fully faithful.

For the essential surjectivity, note that we have to check that a filtration is bounded if and only if it is fpqc hyperlocally. But this follows immediately from the definition of a hypercovering since
A\rightarrow A_{0}
has to be an fpqc covering, and thus if a filtration is bounded on the hypercovering, it certainly is on
A
. Also, we have to check that the induced morphism on the graded pieces (up to Frobenius twist) is an equivalence if and only if it is so fpqc hyperlocally, but again this follows from the descent of modules.
∎

Recall that the functor
(-)^{\simeq}
that sends an
\infty
-category to its underlying Kan complex is right adjoint to the inclusion and thus preserves limits. In particular, the hypercomplete sheaf
\FZip_{\infty,R}
induces a derived stack. We want to show that this stack is locally geometric. To do so, we have to write it as a filtered colimit of geometric stacks. In the case of perfect modules, we restricted ourselves to perfect modules of fixed Tor-amplitude
\textup{Perf}_{R}^{[a,b]}
. To use the geometricity of
\textup{Perf}_{R}^{[a,b]}
to our advantage, we fix the Tor-amplitude of the graded pieces associated to the descending filtration of a derived
F
-zip
(C^{\bullet},D_{\bullet},\phi,\varphi_{\bullet})
. By the boundedness of the filtrations, this also fixes the Tor-amplitude of each
C^{i}
and
D_{i}
for all
i\in\mathbb{Z}
(for
D_{i}
we use the equivalences given by
\varphi_{\bullet}
). But this is still not enough for geometricity since we would need to cover filtrations that could get bigger and bigger. To solve this problem, we also fix a finite subset
S\subseteq\mathbb{Z}
, where the
i^{\mathrm{th}}
graded piece vanishes for
i\notin S
. This is analogous to fixing the
type
(see Definition
3.31
), which is done in the classical setting by
[
MW04
]
. This approach also works, as seen later in Remark
3.44
, but amounts to the same proof.

Definition 3.24
.

We define the
derived stack of
F
-zips

\displaystyle\FZip_{R}\colon\textup{AR}_{R}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\mathbb{S}

\displaystyle A

\displaystyle\longmapsto\FZip_{\infty,R}(A)^{\simeq}.

For a finite subset
S\subseteq\mathbb{Z}
and
a\leq b\in\mathbb{Z}
, we define
\FZip_{R}^{[a,b],S}
as the derived substack over
\FZip_{R}
, where we restrict ourselves to the
F
-zips
(C^{\bullet},D_{\bullet},\phi,\varphi_{\bullet})
such that
\gr^{i}C\simeq 0
for
i\notin S
and the Tor-amplitude of
\gr^{i}C
is contained in
[a,b]
for all
i\in S
(note that both conditions can be tested locally, and thus
\FZip_{R}^{[a,b],S}
indeed defines a derived substack).

Let us quickly insert a remark that is needed for the proof of the next theorem.

Remark 3.25
.

Let us look at the following diagram in
\mathbb{S}
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W\hbox to0.0pt{.\hss}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}

Let us assume this diagram is a pullback diagram in
\mathbb{S}
. If one of the morphisms
a
or
b
is a Kan fibration, then
Z
is equivalent in
\mathbb{S}
to the ordinary pullback in the category of simplicial sets. This is a standard result for homotopy pullbacks in model categories (see
[
Lur09
, Remark A.2.4.5]
).

If we replace
\mathbb{S}
with
\textup{Cat}_{\infty}
, where we also replace “Kan fibration” with “isofibration”, the same holds after applying
(-)^{\simeq}\colon\textup{Cat}_{\infty}\rightarrow\mathbb{S}
to the diagram. A more detailed overview of this can also be found in
[
Yay22b
, Remark 4.13]
.

An important example of a Kan fibration (resp. an isofibration) is the naturally induced morphism
\Fun(B,X)\rightarrow\Fun(A,X)
for a simplicial set
B
, a simplicial subset
A\subseteq B
and a Kan complex (resp. an
\infty
-category)
X
(see
[
Lur24
, 00TJ, 01BS]
; again, a more details can also be found in
[
Yay22b
, Remark 4.13]
).

Theorem 3.26
.

Let
S\subseteq\mathbb{Z}
be a finite subset and
a\leq b\in\mathbb{Z}
. The derived stack
\FZip_{R}^{[a,b],S}
is
(b-a+1)
-geometric and locally of finite presentation. Further, the functor
\FZip^{[a,b],S}_{R}\rightarrow\textup{Perf}^{\,[a,b]}_{R}
induced by
(C^{\bullet},D_{\bullet},\phi,\varphi_{\bullet})\mapsto\mathop{\textup{colim}}_{\mathbb{Z}^{\op}}C^{\bullet}
is locally of finite presentation.

We want to note that in fact
\FZip
is locally geometric, which will be shown later on (see Theorem
3.45
).

The idea of the proof is straightforward. We know that perfect modules with fixed Tor-amplitude, morphisms between those and stacks classifying equivalences between those are geometric. Since the filtrations have finite length, we can see them as finite chains between perfect modules with fixed Tor-amplitude, which are also geometric. The only thing left is to extend finite chains of perfect modules to functors from
\mathbb{Z}
(resp.
\mathbb{Z}^{\op}
) to perfect modules with fixed degree where the graded pieces do not vanish. But this is also straightforward since the only thing left is to degenerate each vertex in the finite chain such that it sits in the right degree.

Proof of Theorem
3.26
.

Let
k
be the number of elements of
S
, and let us index
S
in the following way
\{s_{0}<\dots<s_{k-1}\}
. Let us set
n\coloneqq b-a+1
.
Consider the pullback square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Fun\left(\Delta^{1},\MMod^{\perf}_{(-)}\right)^{\simeq}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textup{Perf}^{\,[a,b]}_{R}\times_{R}\textup{Perf}^{\,[a,b]}_{R}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Fun\left(\partial\Delta^{1},\MMod^{\perf}_{(-)}\right)^{\simeq}\hbox to0.0pt{.\hss}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}

Then
V
is an
n
-geometric stack locally of finite presentation, since it classifies morphisms between perfect complexes with Tor-amplitude in
[a,b]
. Thus, the fiber under
p
of a point
\Spec(A)\rightarrow\textup{Perf}^{\,[a,b]}_{R}\times_{R}\textup{Perf}^{\,[a,b]}_{R}
, classified by perfect
A
-modules
(P,Q)
of Tor-amplitude in
[a,b]
, is given by
F_{P\otimes Q^{\vee}}
. This is an
(n-2)
-geometric derived stack and is locally of finite presentation by Lemma
2.56
. Hence, using that the product
\textup{Perf}^{\,[a,b]}_{R}\times_{R}\textup{Perf}^{\,[a,b]}_{R}
is
n
-geometric (since
\textup{Perf}^{\,[a,b]}_{R}
is
n
-geometric by Theorem
2.57
), we see that
V
is
n
-geometric.

We will now glue copies of
V
together so that we can classify a chain of morphisms, and since
F
-zips have two filtrations, we will do this twice. Let us start with
\codom\colon V\rightarrow\textup{Perf}^{\,[a,b]}_{R}
, which sends a morphism to its codomain. This morphism is
n
-geometric and locally of finite presentation since it is the composition of
V\rightarrow\textup{Perf}^{\,[a,b]}_{R}\times_{R}\textup{Perf}^{\,[a,b]}
and
p_{2}\colon\textup{Perf}^{\,[a,b]}_{R}\times_{R}\textup{Perf}^{\,[a,b]}_{R}\rightarrow\textup{Perf}^{\,[a,b]}_{R}
, which both are
n
-geometric and locally of finite presentation (analogously, the map
\dom\colon V\rightarrow\textup{Perf}^{\,[a,b]}
which sends a morphism to its domain is
n
-geometric and locally of finite presentation). In particular,
\widetilde{V_{1}}\coloneqq V\times_{\codom,\textup{Perf}^{\,[a,b]}_{R},\codom}V
is
n
-geometric and locally of finite presentation.

The derived stack
\widetilde{V_{1}}
classifies tuples of morphisms of perfect modules
(M\rightarrow M^{\prime},N\rightarrow N^{\prime})
such that
M^{\prime}
is equivalent to
N^{\prime}
. This is not an extra datum, as
\codom
from
V
to
\textup{Perf}_{R}^{[a,b]}
is pointwise a Kan fibration, so we can use Remark
3.25
to see that
\widetilde{V_{1}}
is pointwise equivalent in
\mathbb{S}
to the ordinary pullback of simplicial sets, where we do not need to keep track of the equivalence of
M^{\prime}
and
N^{\prime}
.
Since we want to keep track of the equivalence between the colimits, we define
V_{1}
via the pullback square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Fun\left(\Delta^{1},\textup{Perf}^{\,[a,b]}_{R}\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{V_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Fun\left(\partial\Delta^{1},\textup{Perf}^{\,[a,b]}_{R}\right)\hbox to0.0pt{.\hss}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{p}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\codom,\codom)}

Note that for any morphism
\Spec(A)\rightarrow\widetilde{V_{1}}
, classified by two morphisms
(M\rightarrow M^{\prime},N\rightarrow N^{\prime})
of perfect
A
-modules of Tor-amplitude
[a,b]
, the fiber under
\widetilde{p}
is given by the stack classifying equivalences between
M^{\prime}
and
N^{\prime}
, which is open in the derived stack
\Hom_{\MMod_{A}}(M^{\prime}\otimes_{A}(N^{\prime})^{\vee},-)
over
A
by Lemma
2.55
and thus
(n-2)
-geometric and locally of finite presentation by Lemma
2.56
. By our construction,
V_{1}
is
n
-geometric and classifies tuples
(f,g,\psi)
, where
f,g
are morphisms of perfect modules and
\psi
is an equivalence between their codomains.

Let us set recursively

V_{l}\coloneqq(V\times_{R}V)\times_{\codom\times\codom,\textup{Perf}^{\,[a,b]}_{R}\times_{R}\textup{Perf}^{\,[a,b]}_{R},\dom\times\dom}V_{l-1}

for
l\geq 2
. Let us also define
V_{0}
as the stack classifying equivalences between perfect modules (analogously defined as
V
). Here
\dom\times\dom\colon V_{l-1}\rightarrow\textup{Perf}_{R}^{\,[a,b]}\times_{R}\textup{Perf}_{R}^{\,[a,b]}
is defined for
l>2
by projecting to
V\times_{R}V
and then further projecting by
\dom\times\dom
, and for
l=2
, it is directly given by
\dom\times\dom
(note that we have two projections from
V_{1}
to
V
). Using the same arguments as before, we see that
V_{l}
is
n
-geometric and locally of finite presentation for all
l\geq 0
. Now let us look at
V_{k-1}
. The stack
V_{k-1}
classifies two chains of length
k-1
of morphisms of perfect modules with Tor-amplitude in
[a,b]
, with an equivalence of the ends of the two chains.

Later in the proof, we will identify
V_{k-1}
with the stack that classifies perfect modules with Tor-amplitude in
[a,b]
and two bounded filtrations with graded pieces of Tor-amplitude in
[a,b]
(one descending, one ascending) with
k
non-trivial graded pieces.

We extend the chains by zero on the left by defining

\widetilde{V_{k-1}}\coloneqq V_{k-1}\times_{\dom\times\dom,\textup{Perf}_{R}^{\,[a,b]}\times_{R}\textup{Perf}_{R}^{\,[a,b]},p_{0}\times p_{0}}W\times_{R}W,

where
W
is defined via the pullback

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Fun\left(\Delta^{1},\MMod^{\perf}_{(-)}\right)^{\simeq}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textup{Perf}^{\,[a,b]}_{R}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Fun\left(\partial\Delta^{1},\MMod^{\perf}_{(-)}\right)^{\simeq}\hbox to0.0pt{.\hss}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M\mapsto(0,M)}

Since
0
is the initial object in perfect modules, we see that
W\simeq\textup{Perf}_{R}^{\,[a,b]}
and thus
\widetilde{V_{k-1}}\simeq V_{k-1}
, but this description will ease the connection to derived
F
-zips (we have to add zeroes to get left-bounded filtrations, and by working with
\widetilde{V_{k-1}}
, this is automatic if we degenerate the leftmost vertex). Note that we can identify an element in
\widetilde{V_{k-1}}(A)
with two functors

C_{\bullet},D_{\bullet}\colon S_{-}\coloneqq\{s_{0}-1<s_{0}<\dots<s_{k-1}\}\longrightarrow\MMod^{\perf}_{A}.

Sending
C_{\bullet}
to the functor

\displaystyle C^{\bullet}\colon S_{+}^{\op}\coloneqq\{s_{0}<\dots<s_{k-1}<s_{k-1}+1\}^{\op}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\MMod^{\perf}_{A}

\displaystyle s_{i}

\displaystyle\longmapsto C_{s_{k-1-i}}

\displaystyle s_{k-1}+1

\displaystyle\longmapsto C_{s_{0}-1}

defines an obvious equivalence between the corresponding functor categories, and so we may see an element
\widetilde{V_{k-1}}(A)
as a tuple
(C^{\bullet},D_{\bullet},\psi)
of a finite descending chain
C^{\bullet}
of perfect
A
-modules, a finite ascending chain
D_{\bullet}
of perfect
A
-modules and an equivalence of their respective colimits,
i.e.

\psi\colon\mathop{\textup{colim}}_{S_{+}^{\op}}C^{\bullet}\simeq\mathop{\textup{colim}}_{S_{-}}D_{\bullet}.

With this identification, we get a morphism from
\widetilde{V_{k-1}}
to the
k
-fold product of
\Fun(\partial\Delta^{1},\textup{Perf})
, by sending a pair of chains to the graded pieces of the chains (resp. if we see them as filtrations, we send them to the non-trivial graded pieces);
i.e.
if the filtrations of an element in
\widetilde{V_{k-1}}(A)
, for some
A\in\textup{AR}_{R}
, are given by
(C^{\bullet},D_{\bullet},\psi)
, we take
((\gr^{s}C)^{(1)},\gr^{s}D)_{s\in S}
(here
\gr^{s}C
is defined as the cofiber of
C^{s_{i+1}}\rightarrow C^{s_{i}}
, where
s_{i}
corresponds to
s
in the notation above, and analogously for
\gr^{s}D
). With this, let us look at the pullback square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{V}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prodop\displaylimits_{s\in S}\Fun(\Delta^{1},\textup{Perf}_{R})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{V_{k-1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prodop\displaylimits_{s\in S}\Fun(\partial\Delta^{1},\textup{Perf}_{R})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces((\gr^{s}(-))^{(1)},\gr^{s}(-))_{s\in S}}

(note the difference with the previous squares: previously, we considered morphisms in
\MMod_{-}^{\perf}
, whereas here we consider morphisms in the underlying Kan complex, so only invertible ones).

Now let
\Spec(A)\rightarrow\widetilde{V_{k}}
be a morphism classified by a perfect
A
-module with Tor-amplitude in
[a,b]
and a descending (resp. ascending) chain
C^{\bullet}
(resp.
D_{\bullet}
). Then
p^{-1}(\Spec(A))
is given by the stack

\prodop\displaylimits_{s\in S}\Equiv((\gr^{s}C)^{(1)},\gr^{s}D),

where
\Equiv((\gr^{s}C)^{(1)},\gr^{s}D)
denotes the stack classifying equivalences between
(\gr^{s}C)^{(1)}
and
\gr^{s}D
. This stack is
n
-geometric and locally of finite presentation (since each term is
n
-geometric and locally of finite presentation)
(17)
(17)

(17)

Again,
\Equiv((\gr^{s}C)^{(1)},\gr^{s}D)
is open in the derived stack
\Hom_{\MMod_{A}}((\gr^{s}C)^{(1)}\otimes(\gr^{s}D)^{\vee},-)
over
A
by Lemma
2.55
, and
\Hom_{\MMod_{A}}((\gr^{s}C)^{(1)}\otimes(\gr^{s}D)^{\vee},-)\rightarrow\Spec(A)
is
(n-2)
-geometric by Lemma
2.56
.
, and therefore we see that
\widetilde{V}
is
n
-geometric and locally of finite presentation.
By construction, we also have that the morphism

\widetilde{V}\longrightarrow\textup{Perf}^{\,[a,b]}_{R}

given by sending one of the chains to its colimit is
n
-geometric and locally of finite presentation (as it is given by projections down to
\textup{Perf}^{\,[a,b]}_{R}
, which are all
n
-geometric and locally of finite presentation).

We can naturally define a functor
F\colon\widetilde{V}\rightarrow\FZip^{[a,b],S}_{R}
by extending the filtrations via the identity to get non-trivial graded pieces at the points of
S
and the isomorphisms of the graded pieces by the essentially unique zero morphism.

To be more specific, we will show how to give
F
as a functor of simplicial sets. The extension by the identity will just be degeneration of simplicial sets. Let
\sigma\in\widetilde{V}(A)
be an
m
-simplex. Then
\sigma
is given by functors

\displaystyle D\colon S_{-}\times\Delta^{m}\longrightarrow\MMod^{\perf}_{A},\quad C\colon S_{+}^{\op}\times\Delta^{m}\longrightarrow\MMod^{\perf}_{A}

with

\psi\colon C_{|\{s_{0}\}\times\Delta^{m}}\simeq D_{|\{s_{k-1}\}\times\Delta^{m}},\quad C_{|\{s_{k-1}+1\}\times\Delta^{m}}\simeq 0\simeq D_{|\{s_{0}-1\}\times\Delta^{m}}

and
(\varphi_{s})_{s\in S}
, where
\varphi_{s}\colon\Delta^{1}\times\Delta^{m}\rightarrow\textup{Perf}_{R}(A)
is a functor between simplicial sets, such that

\varphi_{s|\Delta^{\{0\}}\times\Delta^{\{i\}}}\simeq(\gr^{s}C)^{(1)}\quad\textup{and}\quad\varphi_{s|\Delta^{\{1\}}\times\Delta^{\{i\}}}\simeq\gr^{s}D.

We will show how to define an extension of
D
,
i.e.
a functor
\bar{D}\colon\mathbb{Z}\times\Delta^{m}\rightarrow\MMod^{\perf}_{A}
, that restricted to
S_{-}
is equivalent to
D
.

Let
(\alpha,\sigma)
be an
l
-simplex in
\mathbb{Z}\times\Delta^{m}
; note that
\alpha
is given by a sequence of integers
\alpha_{0}\leq\dots\leq\alpha_{l-1}
. We are going to count the number of
\alpha_{i}
that are between two vertices in
S_{-}
and degenerate our finite chain for this amount. This can be thought of as adding the right amount of identities between vertices so that the resulting filtration has non-vanishing graded pieces precisely at all
s\in S
.

Let us denote the degeneracy maps of
S_{-}
by
\lambda_{\bullet}
(here we see the finite ordered set
S_{-}
as an
\infty
-category and set
\lambda_{-\infty}
as the degeneracy map corresponding to
s_{0}-1
). For
s_{d}\in S_{-}
, define sets
A_{d}\coloneqq\{\alpha_{j}\mid s_{d}\leq\alpha_{j}\leq s_{d+1}\}
, where we set
s_{k}=\infty
and
A_{-\infty}\coloneqq\{\alpha_{j}\mid\alpha_{j}\leq s_{0}-1\}
. Let us further set
n_{i}=\#A_{i}
. We consider the
l
-simplex in
S_{-}
of the form

{\bar{\alpha}}\coloneqq\lambda^{n_{k-1}-1}_{k-1}\circ\dots\circ\lambda^{n_{-\infty}-1}_{-\infty}\langle s_{j}\mid A_{j}\neq\emptyset\rangle,

where
\lambda_{i}^{-1}\coloneqq\id
. Thus, we can set
\bar{D}(\alpha,\sigma)\coloneqq D({\bar{\alpha}},\sigma)
. Similarly, this can be done to extend
C
,
\psi
and
(\varphi_{s})_{s}
, which defines the functor
F
.

We also get a projection
P\colon\FZip^{[a,b],S}_{R}\rightarrow\widetilde{V}
via restricting the filtrations to
\{s_{0}-1\leq s_{0}\leq\dots\leq s_{k-1}\}\simeq S_{-}
, resp.
\{s_{0}\leq\dots\leq s_{k-1}\leq s_{k-1}+1\}\simeq S_{+}
, and the equivalences to
\Fun(\Delta^{1},\Fun(S^{\disc},\textup{Perf}_{R}(A)))
. Since by this construction
P\circ F
is the identity, we see that
F
is in fact a monomorphism, and since it is an effective epimorphism (by Remark
3.17
,
F
is pointwise essentially surjective on the level of homotopy categories), this shows that
F
is an equivalence of derived stacks.
∎

Corollary 3.27
.

Let
S\subseteq\mathbb{Z}
be a finite subset and
a\leq b\in\mathbb{Z}
. The derived stack
\FZip^{[a,b],S}_{R}
has a perfect cotangent complex .

Proof.

This follows from Theorem
3.26
and Corollary
2.50
.

∎

3.3. On some substacks of
\FZip

In this section, we want to define the
type
of a derived
F
-zip and look at the derived substacks classified by the type. We do the same with those derived
F
-zips where the underlying module has some fixed Euler characteristic. These derived substacks will be open (resp. locally closed), and we will use these to write the derived stack of derived
F
-zips as a filtered colimit of open derived substacks.

We do this in the spirit of the classical theory of
F
-zips over a scheme
S
. There, the type of a classical
F
-zip
(M,C^{\bullet},D_{\bullet},\varphi_{\bullet})
is a function from
S
to functions
\mathbb{Z}\rightarrow\mathbb{N}_{0}
with finite support that assigns to a point
s\in S
the function
k\mapsto\dim_{\kappa(s)}(\gr^{k}_{C}M\otimes_{{\mathcal{O}}_{S}}\kappa(s))
(one uses that the graded pieces of a classical
F
-zip are finite projective and thus the dimension on the fibers is locally constant with respect to
s
).

Since we are working with complexes, we have to modify the definition of the type. To be more specific, we will look at fiberwise dimensions of all cohomologies at once. For a perfect complex
P
over
S
, the assignment
s\in S\mapsto H^{i}(P\otimes_{{\mathcal{O}}_{S}}\kappa(s))
defines an upper semi-continuous function. Since derived
F
-zips have only finitely many non-zero graded pieces, we will use this result to analyze the geometry of the derived substacks classifying derived
F
-zips with certain type.

Definition and Remark 3.28
.

Let
A
be an animated ring, and let
P
be a perfect
A
-module. We define the function

\displaystyle\beta_{P}\colon\Spec(\pi_{0}A)_{\textup{cl}}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\mathbb{N}_{0}^{\mathbb{Z}}

\displaystyle a

\displaystyle\longmapsto(\dim_{\kappa(a)}\pi_{i}(P\otimes_{A}\kappa(a)))_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}.

Since
P\otimes_{A}\pi_{0}A
is perfect and thus has bounded Tor-amplitude, we see that
\beta_{P}
takes values in functions
\mathbb{Z}\rightarrow\mathbb{N}_{0}
with finite support and
\beta_{P}^{-1}(([0,k_{i}])_{i\in\mathbb{Z}})
is open and quasi-compact for any
(k_{i})_{i}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{\mathbb{Z}}
(see
Lemma
2.52
).

Let
I\subseteq\mathbb{Z}
be a finite subset. Assume that
\textup{Supp}(\beta_{P}(a))\subseteq I
for all
a\in\Spec(\pi_{0}A)_{\textup{cl}}
. Recall from Remark
2.53
that this implies that
P
has Tor-amplitude in
[\min(I),\max(I)]
. Further, as explained in the proof of Lemma
2.54
, Nakayama’s lemma implies that if
\beta_{P}
constant with value equal to
(0)_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}
, then
P\simeq 0
.

The following definition will be used to ease notation.

Definition 3.29
.

Let
f\colon\mathbb{Z}\rightarrow\mathbb{N}_{0}^{\mathbb{Z}}
be a function. We say that
f
has
finite support
if the induced function
\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Z}\rightarrow\mathbb{N}_{0}
given by
(n,m)\mapsto f(n)_{m}
has finite support.

Remark 3.30
.

A function
f\colon\mathbb{Z}\rightarrow\mathbb{N}_{0}^{\mathbb{Z}}
has finite support if the sets

\{n\in\mathbb{Z}\mid f(n)\neq(0)_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}\},\quad\{k\in\mathbb{Z}\mid f(n)_{k}\neq 0\}

are finite.

Definition and Remark 3.31
.

Let
A
be an animated
R
-algebra and
\underline{F}\coloneqq(C^{\bullet},D_{\bullet},\phi,\varphi_{\bullet})
be a derived
F
-zip over
A
. Consider the function

\displaystyle\beta_{\underline{F}}\colon a\longmapsto(k\longmapsto\beta_{\gr^{k}C}(a))

from
\Spec(\pi_{0}A)_{\textup{cl}}
to functions with finite support.

(1)

The function
\beta_{\underline{F}}
is called the
type of the derived
F
-zip
\underline{F}
.

(2)

Let
\tau\colon\mathbb{Z}\rightarrow\mathbb{N}_{0}{Z}
be a function with finite support. We say that
\underline{F}
has type at most
\tau
if for all
a\in\Spec(\pi_{0}A)_{\textup{cl}}
, we have
\beta_{\underline{F}}(a)\leq\tau
.
(18)
(18)

(18)

Again, we view
\beta_{P}(a)
and
\tau
as functions from
\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Z}
to
\mathbb{N}_{0}
and define the inequality pointwise.

Further, for any
a\in\Spec(\pi_{0}A)_{\textup{cl}}
, there exist a quasi-compact open neighbourhood
U_{a}
of
a
(resp. locally closed subset
V_{a}
containing
a
) and a function
\tau\colon\mathbb{Z}\rightarrow\mathbb{N}_{0}^{\mathbb{Z}}
with finite support, such that
\beta_{\underline{F}|U_{a}}\leq\tau
in the above sense (resp.
\beta_{\underline{F}|V_{a}}
is constant and equal to
\tau
); this follows from Lemma
2.52
.

Remark 3.32
.

Let us come back to our example of a proper smooth morphism
f\colon X\rightarrow\Spec(A)
.
Let
\tau\colon\mathbb{Z}\rightarrow\mathbb{N}_{0}^{\mathbb{Z}}
be a function with finite support. Then the derived
F
-zip
\underline{R\Gamma_{\dR}(X/A)}
of Example
3.18
has type at most
\tau
if the Hodge numbers satisfy

\dim_{\kappa(a)}H^{-j-i}(X_{\kappa(a)},{}^{i}_{X_{\kappa(a)}/\kappa(a)})\leq\tau(i)_{j}

for all
i,j\in\mathbb{Z}
and
a\in\Spec(A)
(note that the minus signs in the Hodge numbers appear since we use
homological
notation).

Definition and Remark 3.33
.

Let
A
be an animated
R
-algebra and
\underline{F}\coloneqq(C^{\bullet},D_{\bullet},\phi,\varphi_{\bullet})
be a derived
F
-zip over
A
. Let us look at the function

\displaystyle\chi_{k}(\underline{F})\colon\Spec(\pi_{0}A)_{\textup{cl}}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\mathbb{Z}

\displaystyle s

\displaystyle\longmapsto\chi(\gr^{k}C\otimes_{A}\kappa(s)).

This is a locally constant function (see
[
Sta24
, 0B9T]
). Since the filtrations on derived
F
-zips are bounded, we also know that the function
\chi_{\underline{F}}\colon a\mapsto(k\mapsto\chi_{k}(\underline{F})(a))
is also locally constant as a map from
\Spec(\pi_{0}A)_{\textup{cl}}
to functions
\mathbb{Z}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}
with finite support. We call
\chi_{\underline{F}}
the
Euler characteristic
of
\underline{F}
.

If
\tau\colon\mathbb{Z}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}
is a function with finite support, we say that
\underline{F}
has Euler characteristic
\tau
if
\chi_{\underline{F}}
is constant with value
\tau
.

The reason behind the following definition will become clear later on. One problem will be that we cannot classify
F
-zips of fixed type since the type is only “upper semi-continuous” (we do not explicitly define this notion but hope that the idea is clear from the previous definitions and remarks). This can be resolved when we assume that the homotopies of the graded pieces are finite projective.

Definition 3.34
.

Let
A
be an animated ring.

(1)

Let
M\in\MMod_{A}
be a perfect
A
-module. Fix a map
r\colon\mathbb{Z}\rightarrow\mathbb{N}_{0}
. We call
M

homotopy finite projective of rank
r
if for all schemes
S
and scheme morphisms
f\colon S\rightarrow\Spec(\pi_{0}A)_{\textup{cl}}
, the
{\mathcal{O}}_{S}
-module
\pi_{i}(f^{*}(M\otimes_{A}\pi_{0}A))
is finite locally free of rank
r_{i}
.

(2)

Let
\underline{F}\coloneqq(C^{\bullet},D_{\bullet},\phi,\varphi_{\bullet})
be a derived
F
-zip over
A
and
\tau\colon\mathbb{Z}\rightarrow\mathbb{N}_{0}^{\mathbb{Z}}
be a function with finite support. We say that
\underline{F}
is
homotopy finite projective of type
\tau
if for all
i\in\mathbb{Z}
, we have that
\gr^{i}C
is homotopy finite projective of rank
\tau(i)
.

Remark 3.35
.

By the above definition, if a derived
F
-zip is homotopy finite projective of type
\tau
, for
\tau
as above, then its ascending filtration has type
\tau
.

Definition 3.36
.

Let
A
be an animated
R
-algebra. Let
\tau\colon\mathbb{Z}\rightarrow\mathbb{N}_{0}{Z}
be a function with finite support.

(1)

We define
\FZip^{\leq\tau}_{\infty,R}(A)
to be the subcategory of
\FZip_{\infty,R}(A)
consisting of those derived
F
-zips with type at most
\tau
.

(2)

We define
\FZip^{\tau}_{\infty,R}(A)
to be the subcategory of
\FZip_{\infty,R}(A)
consisting of those derived
F
-zips that are homotopy finite projective of type
\tau
.

The associated functors are denoted by
\FZip^{\leq\tau}_{\infty,R}
and
\FZip^{\tau}_{\infty,R}
, and the associated functors from
\textup{AR}_{R}
to
\mathbb{S}
are denoted by
\FZip^{\leq\tau}_{R}
and
\FZip^{\tau}_{R}
, respectively.

Proposition 3.37
.

The functors
\FZip^{\leq\tau}_{\infty,R}
and
\FZip^{\tau}_{\infty,R}
are hypercomplete fpqc sheaves.
In particular,
\FZip^{\leq\tau}_{R}
and
\FZip^{\tau}_{R}
are derived substacks of
\FZip_{R}
.

Proof.

Using arguments as in the proof of Proposition
3.23
, we only have to show that if
A\rightarrow\widetilde{A}
is faithfully flat, a derived
F
-zip over
A
has type at most
\tau
, respectively is homotopy finite projective of type
\tau
, if and only this holds after base change to
\widetilde{A}
. But by faithful flatness, we know that
\Spec(\pi_{0}\widetilde{A})_{\textup{cl}}\rightarrow\Spec(\pi_{0}A)_{\textup{cl}}
is faithfully flat. Now the definitions involved easily show the claim as we note that for an
A
-module
M
and any commutative diagram of the form

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\kappa(a^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Spec(\pi_{0}\widetilde{A})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\kappa(a)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Spec(\pi_{0}A)\hbox to0.0pt{,\hss}}

where
\kappa(a)
is the residue field of a point
a\in\Spec(\pi_{0}A)
and
\kappa(a^{\prime})
is the residue field of a lift
a^{\prime}\in\Spec(\pi_{0}\widetilde{A})
of
a
(this exists by faithful flatness), we have

\displaystyle\dim_{\kappa(a^{\prime})}\pi_{i}(M\otimes_{A}\widetilde{A}\otimes_{\widetilde{A}}\kappa(a^{\prime}))

\displaystyle=\dim_{\kappa(a^{\prime})}\pi_{i}\left(M\otimes_{A}\kappa(a)\otimes_{\kappa(a)}\kappa(a^{\prime})\right)

\displaystyle=\dim_{\kappa(a^{\prime})}\pi_{i}\left(M\otimes_{A}\kappa(a)\right)\otimes_{\kappa(a)}\kappa(a^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\dim_{\kappa(a)}\pi_{i}\left(M\otimes_{A}\kappa(a)\right),

where we use the flatness of field extensions for the second equality.
∎

Definition 3.38
.

Let
A
be an animated
R
-algebra. Let
\tau\colon\mathbb{Z}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}
be a locally constant function with finite support. We define
\FZip_{\infty,R}^{\chi=\tau}(A)
to be the subcategory of
\FZip_{\infty,R}(A)
classifying those derived
F
-zips
\underline{F}
with
\chi_{\underline{F}}=\tau
.

The associated functor is denoted by
\FZip_{\infty,R}^{\chi=\tau}
, and the associated presheaf from
\textup{AR}_{R}
to
\mathbb{S}
is denoted by
\FZip_{R}^{\chi=\tau}
.

Lemma 3.39
.

The functor
\FZip_{\infty,R}^{\chi=\tau}
is a hypercomplete fpqc sheaf. In particular, the functor
\FZip_{R}^{\chi=\tau}
is a derived substack of
\FZip_{R}
.

Proof.

The proof is completely analogous to that of Proposition
3.37
.
∎

In the following, we want to show that the inclusions of the derived substacks
\FZip^{\leq\tau}
,
\FZip^{\tau}
and
\FZip^{\chi=\tau}
into
\FZip
are in fact geometric. To show the geometricity of
\FZip^{\tau}
, we will need a proposition from the book
[
GW23
]
of Görtz–Wedhorn. This proposition in particular shows the reason behind the definition of
homotopy finite projectiveness
. The finite projectiveness of the homotopy groups is needed to have some geometric structure if we fix the type.

Lemma 3.40

(
cf.

[
GW23
, Proposition 23.130]
)
.

Let
S
be a scheme, let
E
be a perfect complex in
D(S)
of Tor-amplitude
[a,b]
, and let
I\subseteq[a,b]
be an interval containing
a
or
b
. Fix a map
r\colon I\rightarrow\mathbb{N}_{0}
,
i\mapsto r_{i}
. Then there exists a unique locally closed subscheme
j\colon Z=Z_{r}\hookrightarrow S
such that a morphism
f\colon T\rightarrow S
factors through
Z
if and only if for all morphisms
g\colon T^{\prime}\rightarrow T
, the
{\mathcal{O}}_{T^{\prime}}
-module
\pi_{i}(L(f\circ g)^{\ast}E)
is finite locally free of rank
r_{i}
for all
i\in I
. Moreover,

(1)

the immersion
j\colon Z\hookrightarrow S
is of finite presentation;

(2)

as a set, one has

Z=\{s\in S\mid\dim_{\kappa(s)}\pi_{i}(E\otimes^{L}_{{\mathcal{O}}_{S}}\kappa(s))=r_{i}\text{ for all }i\in I\};

(3)

if
f\colon T\rightarrow S
factors as
T\xrightarrow{\bar{f}}Z\xrightarrow{j}S
, then
\pi_{i}(Lf^{*}E\otimes_{{\mathcal{O}}_{T}}^{L}{\mathcal{G}})=\bar{f}^{*}\pi_{i}(j^{*}E)\otimes_{{\mathcal{O}}_{T}}{\mathcal{G}}
for all
i\in I
and for all quasi-coherent
{\mathcal{O}}_{T}
-modules
{\mathcal{G}}
.

We are going to show that derived
F
-zips of certain type are classified by open (resp. locally closed) substacks of
\FZip_{R}
. We have seen in Remark
2.28
that an open immersion
U\hookrightarrow\Spec(\pi_{0}A)
of
R
-algebras, where
A\in\textup{AR}_{R}
, can be lifted to an open immersion
\widetilde{U}\hookrightarrow\Spec(A)
. Also, any morphism
\Spec(T)\rightarrow\Spec(A)
factors through
\widetilde{U}
if étale locally
\Spec(\pi_{0}T)\rightarrow\Spec(\pi_{0}A)
factors through
U
.

We could now try to do the same for closed immersions. So, for a closed immersion, let us say induced by an element
a\in\pi_{0}A
, so of the form
\Spec(\pi_{0}A/(a))\rightarrow\Spec(\pi_{0}A)
, we get a closed immersion
\Spec(A\!\sslash\!\!(a))\rightarrow\Spec(A)
, where
A\!\sslash\!\!(a)
is the derived quotient.
(19)
(19)

(19)

The derived quotient is defined in the following way. An element in
a\in\pi_{0}A=\pi_{0}{}^{\infty}A
gives rise to an element
f\in\Hom(\mathbb{Z}[X],A)\simeq{}^{\infty}A
. Thus we can define the derived quotient as
A\!\sslash\!\!(a)\coloneqq A\otimes_{f,\mathbb{Z}[X],X\mapsto 0}\mathbb{Z}.
But it is not clear that a morphism
\Spec(T)\rightarrow\Spec(A)
factors through
\Spec(A\!\sslash\!\!(a))
if and only it does étale locally on
\pi_{0}
. In particular, in the following proposition we cannot show that
\FZip^{\tau}\hookrightarrow\FZip^{\leq\tau}
is a locally closed immersion; we can only do so after restricting the functors to
R
-algebras (we can even show that it is open and closed).

Remark 3.41
.

In the next proposition, we will analyze the geometric structure of the inclusion
i\colon\FZip_{R}^{\leq\tau}\hookrightarrow\FZip_{R}
. For this, we want to understand the pullback

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Spec(A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\FZip_{R}^{\leq\tau}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\FZip_{R}\hbox to0.0pt{,\hss}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}

where
\Spec(A)\rightarrow\FZip_{R}
is given by some derived
F
-zip
\underline{F}\coloneqq(C^{\bullet},D_{\bullet},\phi,\varphi_{\bullet})
. As explained in Remark
3.17
, we can find an equivalence between
\underline{F}
and a derived
F
-zip
\underline{G}\coloneqq(C^{\prime\bullet},D^{\prime}_{\bullet},\phi^{\prime},\varphi^{\prime}_{\bullet})
, where
\phi^{\prime}
is given by the identity on
\mathop{\textup{colim}}_{\mathbb{Z}^{\op}}C^{\prime\bullet}
. This equivalence between
\underline{F}
and
\underline{G}
defines homotopies

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Spec(A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Spec(A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\FZip_{R}\hbox to0.0pt{,\hss}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{G}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\id_{\Spec(A)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{F}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Spec(A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Spec(A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\FZip_{R}\hbox to0.0pt{.\hss}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\id_{\Spec(A)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{G}}

So, in particular, the pullbacks
\FZip_{R}^{\leq\tau}\times_{i,\FZip_{R},\underline{F}}\Spec(A)
and
\FZip_{R}^{\leq\tau}\times_{i,\FZip_{R},\underline{G}}\Spec(A)
are equivalent. Thus, for geometric properties of the inclusion
i
, we may only work with derived
F
-zips of the form
\underline{G}
(
i.e.
those derived
F
-zips where the equivalence between the colimits is given via the identity).

The same reasoning also works for other substacks.

Proposition 3.42
.

Let
\tau\colon\mathbb{Z}\rightarrow\mathbb{N}_{0}{Z}
be a function with finite support. Then the inclusion
i\colon\FZip_{R}^{\leq\tau}\hookrightarrow\FZip_{R}
is a quasi-compact open immersion.
Further, let
p\colon\FZip_{R}^{\tau}\hookrightarrow\FZip^{\leq\tau}_{R}
denote the inclusion. Then
t_{0}p
is a closed immersion locally of finite presentation.
(
Recall that
t_{0}p
is the restriction of
p
to
R
-algebras by the inclusion
\textup{($R$-Alg)}\hookrightarrow\textup{AR}_{R}
.
)

Proof.

Let
\Spec(A)\rightarrow\FZip_{R}
be a morphism of derived stacks with
A\in\textup{AR}_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}
classified by a derived
F
-zip
\underline{F}=(C^{\bullet},D_{\bullet},\varphi_{\bullet})
.
(20)
(20)

(20)

Keeping Remark
3.41
in mind, we do not need to keep track of the equivalence connecting the colimits of the ascending and descending filtrations.
Then a morphism
f\colon\Spec(T)\rightarrow\Spec(A)
factors through the product
i^{-1}(\underline{F})\coloneqq\Spec(A)\times_{\FZip_{R}}\FZip^{\leq\tau}
if and only if
f^{*}\underline{F}
has type at most
\tau
. By Lemma
2.52
, we know that there is a quasi-compact open subscheme
\widetilde{U}
of
\Spec(\pi_{0}A)
classifying those points of
\Spec(\pi_{0}A)
where
\underline{F}
has type at most
\tau
(note that the filtrations are bounded and that perfect complexes have only finitely many non-zero homotopy groups). We claim that
f
factors through
i^{-1}(\underline{F})
if and only if it factors through the lift
U
of
\widetilde{U}
constructed in Remark
2.28
;
i.e.
if we write
\widetilde{U}=\bigcupop\displaylimits_{i=0}^{n}\Spec(\pi_{0}A_{f_{i}})
, we define
U
as the image of
\coprodop\displaylimits_{i=0}^{n}\Spec(A[f_{i}^{-1}])\rightarrow\Spec(A)
(note that this construction implies that
U
is quasi-compact).

Indeed, it is clear that if
f
factors through
U
, then it certainly factors through
i^{-1}(\underline{F})
. Now assume
f
factors through
i^{-1}(\underline{F})
. In particular, we have

\dim_{\kappa(t)}\pi_{i}(f^{*}\gr^{j}C\otimes_{T}\kappa(t))\leq\tau(j)_{i}

for all
t\in\Spec(\pi_{0}T)
and
i,j\in\mathbb{Z}
. But we have the equalities

\displaystyle\dim_{\kappa(t)}\pi_{i}(f^{*}\gr^{j}C\otimes_{T}\kappa(t))

\displaystyle=\dim_{\kappa(t)}\pi_{i}(\gr^{j}C\otimes_{A}\kappa(t))

\displaystyle=\dim_{\kappa(t)}\pi_{i}\left(\gr^{j}C\otimes_{A}\kappa(\pi_{0}f(t))\otimes_{\kappa(\pi_{0}f(t))}\kappa(t)\right)

\displaystyle=\dim_{\kappa(t)}\pi_{i}\left(\gr^{j}C\otimes_{A}\kappa(\pi_{0}f(t))\right)\otimes_{\kappa(\pi_{0}f(t))}\kappa(t)

\displaystyle=\dim_{\kappa(\pi_{0}f(t))}\pi_{i}\left(\gr^{j}C\otimes_{A}\kappa(\pi_{0}f(t))\right),

where we use the flatness of
\kappa(f(t))\rightarrow\kappa(t)
in the fourth equality. This shows that
\pi_{0}f
factors through
\widetilde{U}
. Let us write
\widetilde{U}=\bigcupop\displaylimits_{j\in J}\Spec((\pi_{0}A)_{f_{j}})_{\textup{cl}}
as a finite union of principal affine opens in
\Spec(\pi_{0}A)
. Then
f
factors though
U=\bigcupop\displaylimits_{j\in J}{\Spec(A[f_{j}^{-1}])}
if and only if there is an étale cover
(T\rightarrow T_{k})_{k\in I}
such that
\Spec(\pi_{0}T_{i})
factors through some
\Spec((\pi_{0}A)_{f_{j}})
(see Remark
2.28
). But this is clear since the base change of
\coprodop\displaylimits_{j\in J}\Spec((\pi_{0}A)_{f_{j}})\rightarrow\widetilde{U}
to
\Spec(\pi_{0}T)
gives an étale cover of
\Spec(\pi_{0}T)
, which can be lifted to an étale cover of
\Spec(T)
(use Proposition
2.12
and note that faithful flatness can be checked on
\pi_{0}
), where this property holds by definition.

For
t_{0}\FZip^{\tau}_{R}
, let
\Spec(A)\rightarrow t_{0}\FZip^{\leq\tau}_{R}
be classified by a derived
F
-zip
\underline{F}
over an
R
-algebra
A
. Then a morphism
f\colon\Spec(T)\rightarrow\Spec(A)
factors through the projection
t_{0}p^{-1}(A)\rightarrow\Spec(A)
if and only if
f^{*}\underline{F}
is homotopy finite projective of type
\tau
. By Lemma
3.40
and the finiteness of the filtrations, we can find a locally closed subscheme
Z
of
\Spec(A)
, where
Z\hookrightarrow\Spec(A)
is finitely presented, such that
f
factors through
Z
if and only if
f^{*}\underline{F}
is homotopy finite projective of type
\tau
. So, in particular,
Z\simeq t_{0}p^{-1}(A)
.

What is left to show is that
Z
is also closed in
\Spec(A)
. Using the upper semi-continuity of the Betti numbers (see Lemma
2.52
), we see that the image of
Z
in
\Spec(A)
is a closed subset, and therefore the immersion
Z\hookrightarrow\Spec(A)
is in fact closed (see
[
Sta24
, 01IQ]
).
∎

Proposition 3.43
.

Let
\tau\colon\mathbb{Z}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}
be a function with finite support. The inclusion
i\colon\FZip^{\chi=\tau}_{R}\hookrightarrow\FZip_{R}
is an open immersion of derived stacks, and further
t_{0}i
is an open and closed immersion.

Proof.

This is completely analogous to the proof of Proposition
3.42
with Remark
3.33
. Nevertheless, we give a proof for completeness.

Let
\Spec(A)\rightarrow\FZip
be a morphism of derived stacks with
A\in\textup{AR}_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}
classified by a derived
F
-zip
\underline{F}=(C^{\bullet},D_{\bullet},\varphi_{\bullet})
. Then a morphism
f\colon\Spec(T)\rightarrow\Spec(A)
factors through
i^{-1}(\underline{F})
if and only if
\chi(f^{*}\underline{F})=\tau
. Since
\chi
is locally constant, we know that we can find an open and closed subscheme
\widetilde{U}
of
\Spec(\pi_{0}A)
classifying those points of
\Spec(\pi_{0}A)
where
\underline{F}
has Euler characteristic
\tau
(note that the filtrations are bounded). Let
U
be the lift of
\widetilde{U}
on
\Spec(A)
constructed in Remark
2.28
. Then by construction,
U
is open in
\Spec(A)
. We claim that
f
factors through
i^{-1}(\underline{F})
if and only if it factors through
U
.

Indeed, if
f
factors through
U
, then certainly it also factors through
i^{-1}(\underline{F})
. Now assume
f
factors through
i^{-1}(\underline{F})
. In particular, we have
\chi(f^{*}\gr^{k}_{C}M\otimes_{T}\kappa(t))=\tau(k)
for all
t\in\Spec(\pi_{0}T)
and
k\in\mathbb{Z}
. But analogously to the proof of Proposition
3.42
, we have

\chi(f^{*}\gr^{k}_{C}M\otimes_{T}\kappa(t))=\chi(f^{*}\gr^{k}_{C}M\otimes_{A}\kappa(\pi_{0}f(t))).

This shows that
\pi_{0}f
factors through
\widetilde{U}
. Now, this factorization can be lifted to a factorization of
f
through
U
(again the argumentation is the same as in the proof of Proposition
3.42
).

That
t_{0}i
is open and closed follows immediately from the above.
∎

Remark 3.44
.

By the above,
\FZip^{\leq\tau}_{R}\hookrightarrow\FZip_{R}
is a
0
-geometric open immersion. In fact, we can see that
\mathop{\textup{colim}}_{\tau}\FZip^{\leq\tau}_{R}\simeq\FZip_{R}
, where
\tau
runs through the functions
\mathbb{Z}\rightarrow\mathbb{N}_{0}{Z}
with finite support. This colimit is filtered, as we can view
\tau
as a function
\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Z}\rightarrow\mathbb{N}_{0}
and get a pointwise order.

For the equivalence, note that any
F
-zip
\underline{F}
has finitely many graded pieces, which all have finite Tor-amplitude, so we can find a function
\sigma\colon\mathbb{Z}\rightarrow\mathbb{N}_{0}^{\mathbb{Z}}
with finite support such that
\underline{F}
has type at most
\sigma
,
i.e.

\underline{F}\in\FZip^{\leq\sigma}
.

Further, the inclusion
\FZip^{\leq\tau}_{R}\hookrightarrow\FZip_{R}
has to factor through some
\FZip^{[a,b],S}_{R}
by the fact that the filtrations are bounded and by Remark
3.28
. By the same arguments as in the proof of Proposition
3.42
, we see that
\FZip^{\leq\tau}_{R}\hookrightarrow\FZip^{[a,b],S}_{R}
is a quasi-compact open immersion, which implies that
\FZip^{\leq\tau}_{R}
itself is
(b-a+1)
-geometric and locally of finite presentation. In particular, we can write
\FZip_{R}
as the filtered colimit of geometric derived open substacks:

\FZip_{R}\simeq\mathop{\lim\limits_{\longrightarrow}}_{\tau}\FZip^{\leq\tau}_{R}.

Theorem 3.45
.

The derived stack
\FZip_{R}
is locally geometric and locally of finite presentation.

Proof.

This follows from Remark
3.44
.
∎

3.4. Strong derived
\boldsymbol{F}
-zips over affine schemes

In the following, we want to look at derived
F
-zips where the underlying ascending and descending filtrations are strong. A morphism between modules is a monomorphism if and only if the induced long exact sequence splits into short exact sequences. Using classical theory, it is not hard to see that restricting to those derived
F
-zips where the underlying filtrations are strong and homotopy finite projective gives an open and closed derived substack of
t_{0}\FZip
. We can also use this open and closed derived substack to easily embed the stack of classical
F
-zips into the derived version via an open immersion.

Definition 3.46
.

Let
A
be an animated
R
-algebra and
\tau\colon\mathbb{Z}\rightarrow\mathbb{N}_{0}^{\mathbb{Z}}
be a function with finite support. We define the full sub-
\infty
-category
\sFZip^{\tau}_{\infty,R}(A)
of
\FZip^{\tau}_{\infty,R}(A)
as consisting of those derived
F
-zips
(C^{\bullet},D_{\bullet},\phi,\varphi)
where the filtrations
C^{\bullet}
and
D_{\bullet}
are in fact strong filtrations. An element in
\sFZip^{\tau}_{\infty,R}(A)
is called a
strong derived
F
-zip over
A
of type
\tau
.

Remark 3.47
.

Let
\tau\colon\mathbb{Z}\rightarrow\mathbb{N}_{0}^{\mathbb{Z}}
be a function with finite support. Let
A
be a classical ring. By definition, it is immediate that the base change along classical rings of strong derived
F
-zips of type
\tau
is again strong of type
\tau
. Therefore, the pullback induces a functor
A\mapsto\sFZip^{\tau}_{\infty,R}(A)
from
(
R
-Alg)
to
\textup{Cat}_{\infty}
(here
(
R
-Alg)
denotes the nerve of the category of
R
-algebras).

Remark 3.48
.

The condition that the underlying filtrations are strong may seem useful, as it turns out, this is a very strong condition (see Theorem
3.74
). This is because monomorphisms of perfect complexes (on a classical ring) with finite projective homotopy groups are automatically
split
