. So a strong filtration is automatically determined by its underlying graded pieces, which indicates that the corresponding spectral sequence should degenerate. But the reason behind the derived
F
-zips is precisely the study of those filtrations with non-degenerate spectral sequences.

In the above definition, we have to fix a type as otherwise it is not clear that strongness is stable under base change. Nevertheless, in this paper we will not encounter strong filtrations that are not homotopy finite projective.

Definition and Proposition 3.49
.

Let
\tau\colon\mathbb{Z}\rightarrow\mathbb{N}_{0}^{\mathbb{Z}}
be a function with finite support.
The presheaf

\displaystyle\sFZip_{\infty,R}^{\tau}\colon\textup{($R$-Alg)}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\textup{Cat}_{\infty}

\displaystyle A

\displaystyle\longmapsto\sFZip^{\tau}_{\infty,R}(A)

is a hypercomplete sheaf for the fpqc topology.

Proof.

Analogously to the proof of Proposition
3.23
, it is enough to show that a morphism of perfect modules with homotopy finite projective cofiber is a monomorphism if and only if it is after passage to an fpqc cover.

Let
A\rightarrow A^{\bullet}
be an fpqc hypercover of an
R
-algebra. For a morphism
f\colon M\rightarrow N
of
A
-modules to be a monomorphism is equivalent to the fact that the natural maps
\pi_{i}N\rightarrow\pi_{i}\cofib(f)
are surjections for all
i\in\mathbb{Z}
. By the flatness of
A\rightarrow A^{\bullet}
, for any
A
-module
L
, we have that
\pi_{i}(L\otimes_{A}A^{\bullet})\cong\pi_{i}(L)\otimes_{A}A^{\bullet}
. Note that by assumption, the
\pi_{i}\cofib(f)
are finite projective and compatible with base change. Thus, we see that it is enough to check that any morphism of classical
A
-modules with finite projective target is surjective if it is so after passage to an fpqc cover. But this is classical (see
[
GW10
, Proposition 8.4]
).
∎

Definition 3.50
.

Let
\tau\colon\mathbb{Z}\rightarrow\mathbb{N}_{0}^{\mathbb{Z}}
be a function with finite support. We define the
derived stack of strong
F
-zips of type
\tau
as

\displaystyle\sFZip^{\tau}_{R}\colon\textup{($R$-Alg)}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\mathbb{S}

\displaystyle A

\displaystyle\longmapsto\sFZip^{\tau}_{\infty,R}(A)^{\simeq}.

Next, we want to show that the stack of strong derived
F
-zips is geometric. This is clear if we know that strong bounded perfect filtrations are open and closed in bounded perfect filtrations (note that the proof of Theorem
3.26
shows that the derived stack of bounded perfect filtrations is geometric). This is an immediate consequence of the following lemma.

Lemma 3.51
.

Let
A
be a
(
classical
)
ring and
M,N
be perfect
A
-modules. Let
{\mathcal{V}}\colon\textup{($A$-Alg)}\rightarrow\mathbb{S}
be the derived stack classifying
A
-module morphisms
f\colon M\rightarrow N
such that
M
and
\cofib(f)
are homotopy finite projective of some types. Further, let
{\mathcal{W}}
be the derived substack defined by those morphisms
f\colon M\rightarrow N
that are monomorphisms. Then the inclusion
{\mathcal{W}}\hookrightarrow{\mathcal{V}}
is an open and closed immersion.

Proof.

The locus where
N\rightarrow\cofib(f)
is surjective is represented by an open subscheme of
\Spec(A)_{\textup{cl}}
(see
[
GW10
, Proposition 8.4]
). In particular,
{\mathcal{W}}\hookrightarrow{\mathcal{V}}
is represented by an open immersion. By the projectivity of the
\pi_{i}(M)
, the locus where
\pi_{i}f
is injective for all
i\in\mathbb{Z}
is equivalently the locus where
\pi_{i}\cofib(f)\rightarrow\pi_{i-1}M
vanishes. But this is closed in
\Spec(A)_{\textup{cl}}
(again by
[
GW10
, Proposition 8.4]
).
∎

Lemma 3.52
.

Let
R
be a ring and
\tau\colon\mathbb{Z}\rightarrow\mathbb{N}_{0}^{\mathbb{Z}}
be a function with finite support. Let
\Fun^{b}_{\tau}(\mathbb{Z},\textup{Perf}_{R})
be the derived stack of perfect filtrations that are homotopy finite projective of type
\tau
. Let

\Fun^{s}_{\tau}(\mathbb{Z},\textup{Perf}_{R})\subseteq t_{0}\Fun_{\tau}(\mathbb{Z},\textup{Perf}_{R})

be the derived substack of strong perfect filtrations that are homotopy finite projective of type
\tau
. Then the inclusion

\Fun^{s}_{\tau}(\mathbb{Z},\textup{Perf}_{R})\xhookrightarrow{\hphantom{aaa}}t_{0}\Fun_{\tau}(\mathbb{Z},\textup{Perf}_{R})

is an open and closed immersion.

The same holds if we replace
\mathbb{Z}
with
\mathbb{Z}^{\op}
.

Proof.

By Lemma
3.51
, the stack classifying monomorphisms between perfect complexes is open and closed. As the filtrations are bounded, this proves the lemma (see Remark
3.28
).
∎

Proposition 3.53
.

Let
\tau\colon\mathbb{Z}\rightarrow\mathbb{N}_{0}^{\mathbb{Z}}
be a function with finite support. The inclusion
\sFZip^{\tau}_{R}\hookrightarrow t_{0}\FZip^{\tau}_{R}
is an open and closed immersion.

Proof.

This follows from Lemma
3.52
.
∎

In the next proposition, we want to show that the stack of classical
F
-zips lies quasi-compact open in
t_{0}\FZip_{R}
. To do so, we fix some type of a classical
F
-zip, let us say
\sigma
(as the classical
F
-zips are the disjoint union of such, this is enough). Then we only look at strong derived
F
-zips where the graded pieces are all finite projective modules sitting in one degree. This can also be achieved by fixing a type
\tau
of the strong derived
F
-zips (see Remark
3.28
). But if we choose
\tau
nicely in relation to
\sigma
, we see that strong derived
F
-zips of type
\tau
are precisely classical
F
-zips of type
\sigma
. Since we only work with derived
F
-zips corresponding to finite projective modules, we thus see the quasi-compact openness of classical
F
-zips in derived ones.

Lemma 3.54
.

Let
\sigma\colon\mathbb{Z}\rightarrow\mathbb{N}_{0}
be a function with finite support. Let us define
\tau^{\sigma}\colon\mathbb{Z}\rightarrow\mathbb{N}_{0}^{\mathbb{Z}}
to be the function given by
k\mapsto\tau^{\sigma}(k)_{0}=\sigma(k)
and
\tau^{\sigma}(k)_{j}=0
for
j\neq 0
.
Let
\textup{cl}\FZip_{R}
denote the classical stack of
F
-zips. Then the inclusion
\textup{cl}\FZip^{\sigma}_{R}\hookrightarrow t_{0}\FZip_{R}
is a quasi-compact open.

Proof.

We know that
t_{0}\FZip_{R}^{\leq\tau^{\sigma}}
is open in
t_{0}\FZip_{R}
, but by our construction, for an element
\underline{F}=(C^{\bullet},D_{\bullet},\varphi_{\bullet})
given by
\Spec(A)\rightarrow t_{0}\FZip_{R}^{\leq\tau^{\sigma}}
, we have that
\gr^{k}C
is equivalent to a finite projective module sitting in degree
0
. In particular, the function
\beta_{\gr^{k}C}
is locally constant. Therefore, even
t_{0}\FZip_{R}^{\tau^{\sigma}}
is quasi-compact open in
t_{0}\FZip_{R}
. Now we have an equivalence
\textup{cl}\FZip_{R}^{\sigma}\simeq t_{0}\sFZip_{R}^{\tau^{\sigma}}
, concluding the proof using Proposition
3.53
.
∎

3.5. Globalization

3.5.1. Globalization of derived stacks

In the following, we want to look at derived
F
-zips over derived schemes. We also want to look at some properties of the corresponding sheaf. Important here is that derived stacks take
affine derived schemes
as parameters and not
derived schemes
. Let us show how to fix this.

Let
R
be a ring. We start by extending a derived stack
X\colon\textup{AR}_{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{S}
via right Kan extension to a presheaf
\mathbb{R}X\colon{\mathcal{P}}(\textup{AR}_{R}^{\op})^{\op}\rightarrow\mathbb{S}
. Using Remark
2.41
, we see that
\mathbb{R}X
is in fact an étale sheaf. In particular, since every derived scheme has an open cover by affines,
\mathbb{R}X_{|\dSch}
is uniquely determined by
\mathbb{R}X_{|\textup{AR}_{R}}\simeq X
.

In the case of derived
F
-zips, we could finish this section with the arguments above. But we can also define derived
F
-zips over derived schemes analogously to Definition
3.24
. We will see that this definition agrees with the definition given by right Kan extension.

3.5.2. Filtrations over derived schemes

We want to globalize the construction of derived
F
-zips. We could do this by right Kan extension but also give a direct definition by globalizing filtrations and define derived
F
-zips over derived schemes
analogously to Definition
3.24
. In fact, both definitions will agree (see Lemma
3.65
).

When working with derived schemes, we always assume that our module categories are small;
i.e.
for any animated ring
A
, we assume that
\MMod_{A}
is small. This is because we want to use Proposition
2.39
to see that quasi-coherent modules satisfy descent.

Let us look at the functor from
\textup{AR}_{R}
to
\textup{Cat}_{\infty}
given by
A\mapsto\Fun(\mathbb{Z},\MMod_{A})
. By Lemma
3.22
, we know that this functor satisfies fpqc hyperdescent. So its right Kan extension to derived schemes will still be an fpqc sheaf (see Remark
2.43
). Since
\Fun(\mathbb{Z},-)
commutes with limits (which was used in the proof of Lemma
3.22
), we immediately see that
\mathbb{R}\Fun(\mathbb{Z},\MMod_{-})(S)\simeq\Fun(\mathbb{Z},\QQCoh(S))
for any derived scheme
S
.

We have that boundedness can be checked fpqc hyperlocally (which was used in the proof of Proposition
3.23
). Thus, we can right Kan extend to derived schemes and get a sheaf we denote by

\Fun^{b}(\mathbb{Z},\QQCoh(-)).

By Proposition
2.42
, we see that for a derived scheme
S
, an element in
\Fun^{b}(\mathbb{Z},\QQCoh(-))
is given by a functor
F\in\Fun(\mathbb{Z},\QQCoh(S))
such that for any affine open
\iota\colon\Spec(A)\hookrightarrow S
, the ascending filtration
\iota^{*}F
is bounded.

Also, we have that a functor
F\in\Fun(\mathbb{Z},\QQCoh(S))
is in
\Fun^{b}(\mathbb{Z},\QQCoh(-))
if and only if there is a flat atlas
(\Spec(A_{i})\xrightarrow{p_{i}}S)_{i\in I}
such that
p_{i}^{*}F
is bounded.

The same argumentation as above also works for perfect filtrations and strong filtrations of some given type (here we have to restrict to classical schemes). Let us note that the above stays true if we replace
\mathbb{Z}
with
\mathbb{Z}^{\op}
(or in general with any
\infty
-category, but we do not need this).

Definition 3.55
.

Let
\tau\colon\mathbb{Z}\rightarrow\mathbb{N}_{0}^{\mathbb{Z}}
be a function with finite support. Let
S
be a derived scheme. An
ascending
(
resp. descending
)
filtration of quasi-coherent modules over
S
is an element
F\in\Fun(\mathbb{Z},\QQCoh(S))
(resp.
F\in\Fun(\mathbb{Z}^{\op},\QQCoh(S)))
.

(1)

We say that
F
is
locally bounded
if
F
lies in
\Fun^{b}(\mathbb{Z},\QQCoh(S))
(resp.
\Fun^{b}(\mathbb{Z}^{\op},\QQCoh(S)))
,

(2)

We say that
F
is
perfect
if
F
lies in
\Fun_{\perf}(\mathbb{Z},\QQCoh(S))
(resp.
\Fun_{\perf}(\mathbb{Z}^{\op},\QQCoh(S)))
.

(3)

Moreover, if
S
is a (classical) scheme, then we say that
F
is
strong of type
\tau
if
F
lies in
\Fun^{s}_{\tau}(\mathbb{Z},\QQCoh(S))
(resp.
\Fun^{s}_{\tau}(\mathbb{Z}^{\op},\QQCoh(S)))
.

Remark 3.56
.

Note that for any derived scheme

S\in{\mathcal{P}}(\textup{AR}_{R}^{\op})
, the
\infty
-categories
\Fun_{\perf}(\mathbb{Z},\QQCoh(S))
and

\Fun^{b}(\mathbb{Z},\QQCoh(S))
(resp.
\Fun^{s}_{\tau}(\mathbb{Z},\QQCoh(S))
) can be seen as full sub-
\infty
-categories of
\Fun(\mathbb{Z},\QQCoh(S))
(resp.
t_{0}\Fun(\mathbb{Z},\QQCoh(S))
) (the same holds with
\mathbb{Z}
replaced by
\mathbb{Z}^{\op}
).

Lemma 3.57
.

Let
\tau\colon\mathbb{Z}\rightarrow\mathbb{N}_{0}^{\mathbb{Z}}
be a function with finite support. Let
S
be a
(
classical
)
scheme and
F
an ascending
(
resp. descending
)
strong filtration of
{\mathcal{O}}_{S}
-modules of type
\tau
. Then for all
i\in\mathbb{Z}
, the morphism
\partial_{i}\colon F(i-1)\rightarrow F(i)

(
resp.
F(i+1)\rightarrow F(i))
is a monomorphism.

Proof.

For all
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, the morphism
\pi_{n}F(i)\rightarrow\pi_{n}\cofib(\partial_{i})
is a morphism of
{\mathcal{O}}_{S}
-modules (this follows from Proposition
2.44
). In particular, surjectivity can be checked Zariski locally (see
[
GW10
, Proposition 8.4]
).
∎

Notation 3.58
.

Let
S
be a derived scheme, and let
{\mathcal{F}}
be an
{\mathcal{O}}_{S}
-module. If
F
is a descending filtration on
{\mathcal{F}}
, we write
F^{k}\coloneqq F(k)
for
k\in\mathbb{Z}
and
F^{\bullet}\coloneqq F
. If
G
is an ascending filtration on
{\mathcal{F}}
, we write
G_{\bullet}
for
G
and denote its points by
G_{k}
.

Definition 3.59
.

Let
S
be a derived scheme, and let
{\mathcal{F}}
be an
{\mathcal{O}}_{S}
-module. Let
F
be an ascending (resp. descending) filtration on
{\mathcal{F}}
. For any
i\in\mathbb{Z}
, we define the
i^{\mathrm{th}}
graded piece of
F
as
\gr^{i}F\coloneqq\cofib(F(i-1)\rightarrow F(i))
(resp.
\gr^{i}F\coloneqq\cofib(F(i+1)\rightarrow F(i))
).

3.5.3. Derived
\boldsymbol{F}
-zips over schemes

Before defining derived
F
-zips for derived schemes, we first have to make sense of the Frobenius of derived schemes. Classically, the Frobenius on a scheme
X
is equivalent to the morphism given by composition
X\rightarrow X^{(1)}\rightarrow X
of the relative Frobenius and the natural map. The points of
X^{(1)}
are given by restriction along the Frobenius. This can be used to define the Frobenius for derived schemes (even for derived stacks) as in the following.

Remark and Definition 3.60
.

Let
X
be a derived scheme over
R
. For an animated
R
-algebra
A
, we have an
R
-morphism
\Spec(A)\rightarrow X^{(1)}\coloneqq X\times_{\Spec(R),\Frob_{R}}\Spec(R)
if and only if there is a morphism
\Spec(A_{\Frob_{R}})\rightarrow X
, where
A_{\Frob_{R}}
is the restriction of
A
along the Frobenius,
(21)
(21)

(21)

Using the Frobenius on
R
, we can restrict any
R
-algebra
A
along the Frobenius
\Frob_{R}\colon R\rightarrow R
;
i.e.

A_{\Frob_{R}}
is the animated
R
-algebra obtained by composing the natural morphism
R\rightarrow A
with
\Frob_{R}
.

i.e.

X^{(1)}(A)\simeq X(A_{\Frob_{R}})
. Also, we have an
R
-algebra map
\Frob_{A}\colon A\rightarrow A_{\Frob_{R}}
. Thus
X(\Frob_{A})
induces a map
F_{X/S}\colon X\rightarrow X^{(1)}
, which we call the
relative Frobenius of
X
. The composition with the projection gives a map
F_{X}\colon X\rightarrow X^{(1)}\rightarrow X
, called the
Frobenius of
X
.

Remark 3.61
.

Let
S
be a classical scheme. As the (nerve of the) category of schemes lies fully in the
\infty
-category of derived schemes, we see by construction that
F_{S}
agrees with the classical Frobenius morphism. This can be tested on points given by
R
-algebras, where it holds by definition.

The definition above also agrees with the definition of the Frobenius on animated
R
-algebras, as we have
\textup{AR}_{R}\simeq\Fun_{\pi}(\Poly_{R}^{\op},\mathbb{S})
(recall that the Frobenius is induced by the Frobenius on polynomial
R
-algebras).

Moreover, this argument shows that the Frobenius morphism on derived schemes defined above is equivalent to the morphism induced by right Kan extension of the Frobenius on animated rings.

Definition 3.62
.

Let
S
be a derived scheme over
R
. A
derived
F
-zip over
S
is a tuple
(C^{\bullet},D_{\bullet},\phi,\varphi_{\bullet})
consisting of

•

a descending locally bounded perfect filtration
C^{\bullet}
of quasi-coherent modules over
S
,

•

an ascending locally bounded perfect filtration
D_{\bullet}
of quasi-coherent modules over
S
,

•

an equivalence
\phi\colon\mathop{\textup{colim}}_{\mathbb{Z}^{\op}}C^{\bullet}\simeq\mathop{\textup{colim}}_{\mathbb{Z}}D_{\bullet}
and

•

a family of equivalences
\varphi_{k}\colon F_{S}^{*}\gr^{k}C\xrightarrow{\vbox to0.0pt{\vss\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sim$}\vskip-2.0pt}}\gr^{k}D
.

The
\infty
-category of
F
-zips over
S
,
i.e.
the full subcategory of

\displaystyle\left(\Fun_{\perf}(\mathbb{Z}^{\op},\QQCoh(S))\right.

\displaystyle\left.\times_{\mathop{\textup{colim}},\QQCoh(S),\mathop{\textup{colim}}}\Fun_{\perf}(\mathbb{Z},\QQCoh(S))\right)

\displaystyle\times_{\prodop\displaylimits{Z}\Fun(\partial\Delta^{1},\QQCoh(S))}\prodop\displaylimits{Z}\Fun(\Delta^{1},\QQCoh(S))

consisting of
F
-zips, is denoted by
\FZip_{\infty,R}(S)
.

For a morphism
S^{\prime}\rightarrow S
of derived schemes over
R
, we have an obvious base change functor
\FZip_{\infty,R}(S)\rightarrow\FZip_{\infty,R}(S^{\prime})
via the pullback.

Remark 3.63
.

By definition, for any affine derived scheme
\Spec(A)
, the
\infty
-category
\FZip_{\infty,R}(\Spec(A))
indeed recovers Definition
3.24
.

Also, as in Remark
3.17
, on affine schemes, up to equivalence we may assume that the equivalence between the colimits of the filtrations of a derived
F
-zip is given by the identity.

Remark 3.64
.

Note that if we have a locally bounded perfect filtration
C^{\bullet}
over some derived scheme
S
, we have that its colimit in
\QQCoh(S)
is actually perfect. This can be checked Zariski locally, where the filtrations actually become bounded (note that the colimit is filtered), so the colimit can be taken over a finite subcategory of
\mathbb{Z}
and thus is perfect.

The next lemma shows that the
\infty
-category of derived
F
-zips satisfies fpqc descent and that we can extend the definition of derived
F
-zips to arbitrary derived (pre-)stacks.

Lemma 3.65
.

Let
\mathbb{R}\FZip_{\infty,R}
be the the right Kan extension of
\FZip_{\infty,R}\colon\textup{AR}_{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{S}
along the Yoneda embedding
\textup{AR}_{R}\hookrightarrow{\mathcal{P}}(\textup{AR}_{R}^{\op})^{\op}
. Then for any derived scheme
S
over
R
, the natural morphism induced by base change

\FZip_{\infty,R}(S)\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}\FZip_{\infty,R}(S)

is an equivalence.

Proof.

Affine locally on
S
, the assertion is certainly true. So it is enough to show that for an affine open cover
(\Spec(A_{i})\hookrightarrow S)_{i\in I}
, we have

\FZip_{\infty,R}(S)\simeq\lim_{\Delta}\FZip_{\infty,R}\left(\check{C}\left(\coprodop\displaylimits\Spec(A_{i})/S\right)_{\bullet}\right).

This is again completely analogous to the proof in the affine case (see Proposition
3.23
), where we embedded derived
F
-zips into a larger category that satisfied descent. Here we have to use that

\displaystyle S\mapsto\Fun_{\perf}(\mathbb{Z}^{\op},\QQCoh(-))

\displaystyle\times_{\mathop{\textup{colim}},\QQCoh(-),\mathop{\textup{colim}}}\Fun_{\perf}(\mathbb{Z},\QQCoh(S))

\displaystyle\times_{\prodop\displaylimits{Z}\Fun(\partial\Delta^{1},\QQCoh(-))}\prodop\displaylimits{Z}\Fun(\Delta^{1},\QQCoh(S))

is given by the limit of right Kan extensions (see the discussion in Section
3.5.2
) of sheaves and Remark
2.41
.
∎

Lemma
3.65
allows us to globalize the derived stack of derived
F
-zips and gives us a direct description of its points.

Definition and Remark 3.66
.

We define

\FZip_{R}\colon{\mathcal{P}}(\textup{AR}_{R})^{\op}\longrightarrow\mathbb{S}

as the right Kan extension of
\FZip\colon\textup{AR}_{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{S}
along the inclusion
\textup{AR}_{R}\hookrightarrow{\mathcal{P}}(\textup{AR}_{R})^{\op}
.

By Remark
2.41
, we see that
\FZip
is a hypercomplete fpqc sheaf.

Further, for any derived
R
-scheme
S
, we have that
\FZip_{R}(S)\simeq\FZip_{\infty,R}(S)^{\simeq}
by Lemma
3.65
as
(-)^{\simeq}
commutes with limits.

Example 3.67
.

Let us globalize Example
3.18
. Let
f\colon X\rightarrow S
be a proper smooth morphism of schemes. Again, the associated Hodge and conjugate filtrations
HDG
and
conj
define, respectively, a descending and an ascending perfect bounded filtration of quasi-coherent modules over
S
. We also have equivalences
\varphi_{n}\colon F^{*}_{S}\gr^{n}\textup{{HDG}}\xrightarrow{\vbox to0.0pt{\vss\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sim$}\vskip-2.0pt}}\gr^{n}\textup{{conj}}
between the graded pieces (up to Frobenius twist), induced by the Cartier isomorphism. Therefore, we get a derived
F
-zip associated to the proper smooth map
f
of schemes

\underline{Rf_{*}{}^{\bullet}_{X/S}}\coloneqq(\textup{{HDG}}^{\bullet},\textup{{conj}}_{\bullet},\varphi_{\bullet}).

Example 3.68
.

The above construction works analogously for log smooth scheme morphisms (
i.e.
schemes with a fine log structure as explained in
[
Kat89
]
).

If
f\colon X\rightarrow S
is a proper log smooth morphism of Cartier type (note that
f
is by definition integral and thus flat; see
[
Kat89
, Corollary 4.5]
), then
{}^{1}_{X/S}
(the sheaf of log differentials) is locally free of finite rank (see
[
Kat89
, Proposition 3.10]
), and because
f
is proper and flat, the associated Hodge filtrations
\textup{{HDG}}_{\log}
are perfect (use the distinguished triangle associated to the stupid truncation and conclude via induction and the fact that
f
is proper, locally of finite presentation and flat; see
[
Sta24
, 0B91]
; this is analogous to the proof of
[
Sta24
, 0FM0]
). We also have a Cartier isomorphism in this setting (see
[
Kat89
, Theorem 4.12]
) (this implies, using the distinguished triangles for the conjugate filtration
\textup{{conj}}_{\log}
and induction, that the conjugate filtration is perfect). Hence, we have equivalences
\varphi_{n}\colon F^{*}_{S}\gr^{n}\textup{{HDG}}_{\log}\xrightarrow{\vbox to0.0pt{\vss\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sim$}\vskip-2.0pt}}\gr^{n}\textup{{conj}}^{\log}
; thus analogously to the above, we can attach the structure of a derived
F
-zip to
f
via, again,

\left(\textup{{HDG}}^{\bullet}_{\log},\textup{{conj}}_{\bullet}^{\log},\varphi_{\bullet}\right).

Let us consider the notion of strong
F
-zips. The condition that the filtration is given by monomorphisms seems very natural, but as Theorem
3.74
will show, in this case we cannot expect a generalization from classical theory. In particular, the following lemma shows that perfect complexes with finite projective cohomologies are particularly easy to handle.

Lemma 3.69
.

Let
A
be a ring, and let
P
be a perfect complex over
A
such that for all
i\in\mathbb{Z}
, the
A
-module
\pi_{i}(P)
is finite projective. Then there exists a quasi-isomorphism
P\xrightarrow{\vbox to0.0pt{\vss\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sim$}\vskip-2.0pt}}\bigoplusop\displaylimits_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\pi_{n}(P)[n]
.

Proof.

Since
P
is perfect, we may assume that there exist
a\leq b\in\mathbb{Z}
such that
P
has Tor-amplitude in
[a,b]
. We further assume that
P
is represented by the complex of finite projective
A
-modules

\dots\longrightarrow 0\longrightarrow P_{b}\xrightarrow{\,\partial_{b}\,}P_{b-1}\xrightarrow{\partial_{b-1}}\cdots\xrightarrow{\partial_{a+1}}P_{a}\longrightarrow 0\rightarrow\cdots.

Let us define a new complex
P^{\leq a}
given by

\dots\longrightarrow 0\longrightarrow P_{b}\xrightarrow{\,\partial_{b}\,}P_{b-1}\xrightarrow{\partial_{b-1}}\cdots\xrightarrow{\partial_{a+1}}\im(\partial_{a+1})\longrightarrow 0\longrightarrow\cdots.

We get a short exact sequences of complexes

0\longrightarrow P^{\leq a}\longrightarrow P\longrightarrow\pi_{a}(P)[a]\longrightarrow 0.

Since
\pi_{a}(P)
is projective, this induces a section
\pi_{a}(P)\rightarrow P_{a}
, and we can extend this to a morphism
\pi_{a}(P)[a]\rightarrow P
which induces a section of
P\rightarrow\pi_{a}(P)[a]
. Also, this induces a retraction of
P^{\leq a}\rightarrow P
, and, in particular,
P\simeq P^{\leq a}\oplus\pi_{a}(P)[a]
. Now we claim that
P^{\leq a}
is perfect and has Tor-amplitude in
[a+1,b]
, concluding the proof by induction on the Tor-amplitude of
P
.

Indeed, note that
P^{\leq a}
is equivalent to the complex

\cdots\longrightarrow 0\longrightarrow P_{b}\xrightarrow{\,\partial_{b}\,}P_{b-1}\xrightarrow{\partial_{b-1}}\cdots\xrightarrow{\partial_{a}}P_{a+1}\longrightarrow 0\longrightarrow\cdots

which is by construction a complex of finite projective modules concentrated in degrees
[a+1,b]
,
i.e.
a perfect complex of Tor-amplitude in
[a+1,b]
.
∎

We can use Lemma
3.69
to see that a morphism of perfect complexes with finite projective homotopy groups is a split monomorphism if and only if it is so on the cohomologies. This is clear since if the induced map on the cohomologies is injective, then the long exact homotopy sequence corresponding to a fiber sequence consists of short exact sequences. The projectiveness gives us retractions on the level of cohomology groups and thus a retraction on the whole complex.

Remark 3.70
.

Let
A
be a ring, and let
P
and
Q
be a perfect complexes over
A
. Further assume we have a morphism
f\colon P\rightarrow Q
such that for all
i\in\mathbb{Z}
, the
A
-modules
\pi_{i}P
,
\pi_{i}Q
and
\pi_{i}\cofib(f)
are finite projective. Then we claim that
f
is a split monomorphism if and only if
f
is a monomorphism.

Indeed, the ‘‘only if’’ direction is clear.
(22)
(22)

(22)

Let
P\xrightarrow{f}Q\xrightarrow{g}\cofib(f)
be a cofiber sequence of
A
-modules, and let
h\colon\cofib(f)[-1]\rightarrow P
be the naturally induced morphism. By construction, we have
f\circ h\simeq 0
, and as
f
is a monomorphism, this implies
h\simeq 0
and so indeed
\ker(\pi_{i}f)=\im(\pi_{i+1}h)=0
.

For the “if” direction, let
\pi_{i}f
be injective for all
i\in\mathbb{Z}
. By Lemma
3.69
, we may assume that
P\xrightarrow{\vbox to0.0pt{\vss\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sim$}\vskip-2.0pt}}\bigoplusop\displaylimits_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\pi_{n}P[n]
and
Q\xrightarrow{\vbox to0.0pt{\vss\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sim$}\vskip-2.0pt}}\bigoplusop\displaylimits_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\pi_{n}Q[n]
. It is enough to find retractions
g_{i}
of
\pi_{i}f
since then this induces a retraction of
f
.

Since
\pi_{i}f
is injective for all
i\in\mathbb{Z}
, we get short exact sequences

(3.1)

0\rightarrow\pi_{i}P\xrightarrow{\pi_{i}f}\pi_{i}Q\rightarrow\pi_{i}\cofib(f)\rightarrow 0.

As
\pi_{i}\cofib(f)
is projective, we see that the short exact sequence (
3.1
) is split, giving us the retractions of
\pi_{i}f
.

Let
f\colon X\rightarrow S
be a proper smooth morphism. In the following, we want to prove that the derived
F
-zip
\underline{Rf_{*}{}^{\bullet}_{X/S}}
of Example
3.67
is strong if and only if the Hodge–de Rham spectral sequence degenerates and the
{\mathcal{O}}_{S}
-modules
R^{i}f_{*}{}^{j}_{X/S}
are finite locally free.

We first treat the “only if” direction in the case where
S
is locally Noetherian. This is done by proving the case where
S
is a local Artinian ring and then reducing from complete Noetherian rings to local Artinian rings. After this, we insert a lemma showing us that we can reduce to the Noetherian case from the non-Noetherian one. Next, we treat the “‘if” direction more generally, for arbitrary perfect bounded strong filtrations in the derived category and the spectral sequence associated to it.

Proposition 3.71
.

Let
f\colon X\rightarrow S
be a proper smooth morphism of schemes, with
S
locally Noetherian. If the filtrations of the derived
F
-zip
\underline{Rf_{*}{}^{\bullet}_{X/S}}
of Example
3.67
are strong
(
i.e.
HDG
and
conj
are strong
)
, then the Hodge–de Rham spectral sequence degenerates and
R^{i}f_{*}{}_{X/S}^{j}
is finite locally free for all
i,j\in\mathbb{Z}
.

Proof.

Since the question is local, we may assume that
S=\Spec(A)
is a Noetherian affine scheme.

Let us first treat the case where
A
is a local Artinian ring with maximal ideal
{\mathfrak m}
and residue field
k
. We can check the degeneracy of the Hodge–de Rham spectral sequence via comparing lengths of the limit term and the
E_{1}
-terms of the spectral sequence. But in this case this is clear since we get a short exact sequence

0\longrightarrow H^{n}\left(X,\sigma_{\geq i+1}{}_{X/S}^{\bullet}\right)\longrightarrow H^{n}\left(X,\sigma_{\geq i}{}_{X/S}^{\bullet}\right)\longrightarrow H^{n-i}\left(X,{}_{X/S}^{i}\right)\longrightarrow 0

for all
i\geq 0
and
n\in\mathbb{Z}
(by the strongness of our filtration). This implies that

\length_{A}H^{n}\left(X,\sigma_{\geq n}{}_{X/S}^{\bullet}\right)=\length_{A}H^{n}\left(X,\sigma_{\geq i+1}{}_{X/S}^{\bullet}\right)+\length_{A}H^{n-i}\left(X,{}_{X/S}^{n}\right)

for all
i\geq 0
and
n\in\mathbb{Z}
. As
H^{n}_{\dR}(X/S)=H^{n}(X,\sigma_{\geq 0}{}_{X/S}^{\bullet})
, this implies inductively that

\length_{A}H^{n}_{\dR}(X/S)=\sumop\displaylimits_{i\geq 0}\length_{A}H^{n-i}\left(X,{}_{X/S}^{i}\right)=\sumop\displaylimits_{p+q=n}\length_{A}H^{q}\left(X,{}_{X/S}^{p}\right).

Thus, for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, we get the equation

\sumop\displaylimits_{p+q=n}\length_{A}E^{p,q}_{\infty}=\length_{A}H^{n}_{\dR}(X/S)=\sumop\displaylimits_{p+q=n}\length_{A}H^{q}\left(X,{}_{X/S}^{p}\right),

so, in particular, in this case the Hodge–de Rham spectral sequence degenerates.

Analogously, we see that the conjugate spectral sequence also degenerates (again by a length argument using the strongness of the conjugate filtration).
Let us now show, following the arguments of the proof of
[
DI87
, Proposition 4.1.2]
, that the
H^{i}(X,{}^{j}_{X/S})
are finite (locally) free.

Since
f
is proper smooth, we know that
Rf_{*}{}^{j}_{X/S}
is a perfect complex and its formation commutes with arbitrary base change (see
[
Sta24
, 0FM0]
). In particular,
(Rf_{*}{}^{j}_{X/S})\otimes_{A}k\cong Rf_{*}{}^{j}_{X_{k}/k}
. Therefore, we may assume that
Rf_{*}{}^{j}_{X/S}
is given by a complex
K^{j}_{\bullet}
of free
A
-modules
K^{j}_{i}=A^{h^{ij}}
, where
h^{ij}\coloneqq\dim_{k}H^{i}(X,{}^{j}_{X_{k}/k})
(see
[
Sta24
, 0BCD]
). In particular, we see that

\dim_{k}(K^{j}_{\bullet}\otimes_{A}k)=\dim_{k}H^{i}(K^{j}_{\bullet}\otimes_{A}k)=h^{ij}.

Thus, we get the following inequality:

(3.2)

\length_{A}H^{i}\left(X,{}^{j}_{X/S}\right)=\length_{A}H^{i}(K^{j}_{\bullet})\leq\length_{A}K^{j}_{\bullet}=h^{ij}\length_{A}A.

Equality in (
3.2
) holds if and only if the differentials of
K^{j}_{\bullet}
are zero, implying that
H^{i}(X,{}^{j}_{X/S})
is finite (locally) free. The inequality (
3.2
) also shows that equality holds if and only if

(3.3)

\sumop\displaylimits_{i+j=n}\length_{A}H^{i}\left(X,{}^{j}_{X/S}\right)=\sumop\displaylimits_{i+j=n}h^{ij}\length_{A}A.

By the degeneracy of the Hodge–de Rham spectral sequence, the left-hand side of (
3.3
) is equal to
\length_{A}H^{n}_{\dR}(X/S)
. The degeneracy of the conjugate spectral sequence, together with the Cartier isomorphism, implies that

(3.4)

\sumop\displaylimits_{i+j=n}\length_{A}H^{i}\left(X,{}^{j}_{X/S}\right)=\sumop\displaylimits_{i+j=n}\length_{A}H^{i}\left(X^{(1)},{}^{j}_{X^{(1)}/S}\right),

where the notation is induced by the pullback diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{(1)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S\hbox to0.0pt{,\hss}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{X/S}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{(1)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Frob_{S}}

where
\Frob_{S}
denotes the Frobenius of
S
.
We will show that

(3.5)

\sumop\displaylimits_{i+j=n}\length_{A}H^{i}\left(X^{(1)},{}^{j}_{X^{(1)}/S}\right)=\sumop\displaylimits_{i+j=n}h^{ij}\length_{A}A

by induction on the length of
A
, proving the lemma in the local Artinian case.

If
A
is a field, the assertion is trivial. So let us assume that
\length_{A}A=N
and assume the lemma was shown for
N^{\prime}<N
. Let
1\leq N^{\prime}<N
be such that
pN^{\prime}>N
, and let us set
T=\Spec(A/{\mathfrak m}^{N^{\prime}})
. The choice of
N^{\prime}
assures that
\Frob_{S}
factors through
T
. So, we get the following diagram with pullback squares:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{(1)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{T}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S\hbox to0.0pt{.\hss}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{X/S}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{(1)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{T}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Frob_{S}}

By the induction hypothesis,
H^{i}(X_{T},{}_{X_{T}/T}^{j})
is finite locally free of rank
h^{ij}
and commutes with arbitrary base change. Therefore, the base change along
S\rightarrow T
also has this property. Hence,
H^{i}(X^{(1)},{}_{X^{(1)}/S}^{j})
is finite locally free of rank
h^{ij}
, and therefore (
3.5
) is fulfilled. But this shows that (
3.4
) and thus (
3.3
) are fulfilled. So, by the discussion above, we see that
H^{i}(X,{}_{X/S}^{j})
is finite locally free of rank
h^{ij}
, concluding the induction.

Now let us show how to reduce to the case where
A
is a local Artinian ring. As the question is local, we can even assume that
A
is given by a local Noetherian ring (see
[
GW10
, Proposition 7.27]
). By the faithful flatness of completion (see
[
Sta24
, 00MC]
), we can assume that
A
is a complete Noetherian local ring
(A,{\mathfrak m})
.

Let us first show that
H^{i}(X,{}_{X/S}^{j})
is finite locally free and commutes with arbitrary base change. Let us set
A_{n}\coloneqq A/{\mathfrak m}^{n}
and
X_{n}\coloneqq X\otimes_{A}A_{n}
. Then
f_{n}\colon X_{n}\rightarrow\Spec(A_{n})
is proper smooth, and the Hodge filtration is strong. Since
A_{n}
is a local Artinian ring, we know that the Hodge–de Rham spectral sequence relative to
X_{n}/A_{n}
degenerates, the
H^{i}(X_{n},{}^{j}_{X_{n}/A_{n}})
are finite locally free and

H^{i}\left(X_{n},{}^{j}_{X_{n}/A_{n}}\right)\otimes_{A_{n}}A_{n-1}\cong H^{i}\left(X_{n-1}{}^{j}_{X_{n-1}/A_{n-1}}\right)

for
n-1\geq n
for all
n\geq 0
. The theorem of formal functions (see
[
Sta24
, 02OC]
) now implies that

(3.6)

\lim_{n\geq 0}H^{i}\left(X_{n},{}^{j}_{X_{n}/A_{n}}\right)\cong\lim_{n\geq 0}H^{i}\left(X,{}^{j}_{X/A}\right)\otimes_{A}A_{n}.

Using
[
Sta24
, 0D4B]
, we see that the left-hand side of (
3.6
) is finite projective and
H^{i}(X_{n},{}^{j}_{X_{n}/A_{n}})\simeq H^{i}(X,{}^{j}_{X/S})\otimes_{A}A_{n}
. But as
A
is complete and Noetherian and
H^{i}(X,{}^{j}_{X/S})
is finite (by the perfectness of
Rf_{*}{}_{X/S}^{j}
), the right-hand side of (
3.6
) is equal to
Rf_{*}{}_{X/S}^{j}
(see
[
Sta24
, 00MA]
). In particular, it is finite locally free, and its formation commutes with arbitrary base change.
Again, since finite modules over complete Noetherian rings are complete, we see that if the Hodge spectral sequence degenerates for all
S_{n}=\Spec(A/{\mathfrak m}^{n})
, then it degenerates on the limit of all
A/{\mathfrak m}^{n}
, namely
A
(see
[
Sta24
, 00MA]
and note that the
H^{i}(X,{}^{j}_{X/S})
are finite projective, and therefore their formation commutes with arbitrary base change). Hence, we may assume that
A
is a local Artinian ring, which we already discussed at the beginning.
∎

Lemma 3.72
.

Let
f\colon X\rightarrow S
be a proper smooth morphism of schemes, with

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime}}

Proof.

The existence of an affine scheme

with a morphism

and a proper smooth
S_{0}
-scheme
X_{0}
such that the base change of

along

is equal to
f
is standard.
(23)
(23)

(23)

Use
[

, Theorem 10.69]
to find an affine Noetherian

On the other hand, we can show that if the Hodge–de Rham spectral sequence degenerates, then the Hodge filtration is strong. Also, we do not need the particular form of the Hodge filtration, and thus we will show generally that a bounded perfect filtration with degenerate associated spectral sequence is automatically strong. Later on in Section
4
, we will use this result to show that the derived
F
-zips associated to a proper smooth morphism with degenerative Hodge–de Rham spectral sequence and finite projective
E_{1}
-terms is completely determined by the underlying classical
F
-zips. So, for example in the abelian scheme case, the theory of derived
F
-zips gives us no new information and recovers the classical theory by passing to the cohomologies of the filtration (in a suitable sense as explained in Section
4
).

Proposition 3.73
.

Let
A
be a ring and
C^{\bullet}
be a descending bounded perfect filtration of
A
-modules. Assume that the
\pi_{i}\gr^{k}C
are finite projective for all
i,k\in\mathbb{Z}
and that the spectral sequence

E_{1}^{p,q}=\pi_{p+q}\gr^{p}C\Longrightarrow\pi_{p+q}\mathop{\textup{colim}}_{\mathbb{Z}^{\op}}C^{\bullet}

associated to
C^{\bullet}
degenerates. Then the filtration
C^{\bullet}
is strong, and the statement stays true if we replace
C^{\bullet}
with an ascending filtration.

Proof.

For convenience, let us set
M\coloneqq\mathop{\textup{colim}}_{\mathbb{Z}^{\op}}C^{\bullet}
.

It is enough to show
(24)
(24)

(24)

As the
C^{\bullet}
is bounded, there exists an
n
large enough such that
C^{n}\rightarrow M
is an equivalence and thus also a monomorphism. Hence, induction indeed shows that
C^{\bullet}
is strong.
that

•

if for any
k\in\mathbb{Z}
, the natural map
C^{k}\rightarrow M
is a monomorphism, then the map
C^{k+1}\rightarrow C^{k}
is a monomorphism.

Also, we will see that changing a descending filtration to an ascending one only changes the indices in the following proof and thus works similarly.

So let us fix some
k\in\mathbb{Z}
and assume that
C^{k}\rightarrow M
is a monomorphism. The degeneracy of the spectral sequence implies that
E_{1}^{k,q}=\pi_{k+q}\gr^{k}C
is naturally isomorphic to
E_{\infty}^{k,q}
for any
q\in\mathbb{Z}
. By the construction of the spectral sequence (see
[
Lur17
, Section 1.2.2]
), we see that
E_{\infty}^{k,q}\cong\im(h_{q})/\im(g_{q})
, where
h_{q}\colon\pi_{k+q}C^{k}\rightarrow\pi_{k+q}M
and
g_{q}\colon\pi_{k+q}C^{k+1}\rightarrow\pi_{k+q}M
. As
C^{k}\rightarrow M
is a monomorphism, we see that
h_{q}
is injective and therefore
\im(h_{q})\cong\pi_{k+q}C^{k}
. As the filtration on
M
induced by the spectral sequence is bounded and the graded pieces are finite projective by degeneracy, we see that
\pi_{k+q}C^{k}
is finite projective (since it is isomorphic to a filtered piece by the relation
\im(h_{q})\cong\pi_{k+q}C^{k}
). We claim that the morphism
f_{q}\colon\pi_{k+q}C^{k}\rightarrow\pi_{k+q}\gr^{k}C
induced by the fiber sequence

C^{k+1}\longrightarrow C^{k}\longrightarrow\gr^{k}C

is surjective for all
q\in\mathbb{Z}
.

Indeed, recall from
[
Lur17
, Section 1.2.2]
that
E_{r}^{p,q}
is defined by

\im\left(\pi_{p+q}\cofib\big(C^{p+r}\longrightarrow C^{p}\big)\longrightarrow\pi_{p+q}\cofib\left(C^{p+1}\longrightarrow C^{p-r+1}\right)\right).

As the spectral sequence is degenerate, the proof of
[
Lur17
, Proposition 1.2.2.7(2)]
shows that for any
r\geq 2
, we get a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{k+q}\cofib\left(C^{k+r}\longrightarrow C^{k}\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{k+q}\cofib\left(C^{k+1}\longrightarrow C^{k-r+1}\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E_{r-1}^{k,q}\hbox to0.0pt{,\hss}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi_{r}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\vartheta_{r}}

where
\phi_{r}
is surjective and
\vartheta_{r}
is injective. The morphism
\phi_{r}
is induced by the natural morphism
\cofib(C^{k+r}\rightarrow C^{k})\rightarrow\cofib(C^{k+1}\rightarrow C^{k-r+1})
. Since
C^{\bullet}
is bounded, we see that for
r
large enough, we have
\cofib(C^{k+r}\rightarrow C^{k})\simeq C^{k}
. Thus, we get a morphism
\alpha_{q}\colon\pi_{k+q}C^{k}\rightarrow E_{1}^{k,q}=\pi_{k+p}\gr^{k}C
that is surjective, using the
\phi_{r}
and the degeneracy. But by construction,
\alpha_{q}
is induced by the natural map
C^{k}\rightarrow\gr^{k}C
, and hence
\alpha_{q}
agrees with

, showing the desired surjectivity.
∎

Combining all the arguments, we get the connection between the degeneracy of the Hodge–de Rham spectral sequence and the strongness of the Hodge filtration.

Theorem 3.74
.

Let
f\colon X\rightarrow S
be a smooth proper morphism of schemes. Let us consider the Hodge–de Rham spectral sequence

E_{1}^{p,q}=R^{q}f_{*}{}_{X/S}^{p}\Leftrightarrow R^{p+q}f_{*}{}_{X/S}^{\bullet}.

The filtrations of the derived
F
-zip
\underline{Rf_{*}{}^{\bullet}_{X/S}}
of Example
3.67
are strong
(
in the sense that
HDG
and
conj
are strong
)
if and only if the Hodge–de Rham spectral sequence degenerates and
R^{i}f_{*}{}_{X/S}^{j}
is finite locally free for all
i,j\in\mathbb{Z}

Proof.

The “if” part follows from Proposition
3.73
, where we use that degeneracy of the Hodge–de Rham spectral sequence implies that the conjugate spectral sequence is degenerate (see
[
Kat72
, Proposition (2.3.2)]
;
note that we need that the
R^{i}f_{*}{}_{X/S}^{j}
are finite locally free).

For the “only if” part, we may assume that
S=\Spec(A)
is affine, as the question is local. We can use Lemma
3.72
to find a proper smooth scheme morphism
f^{\prime}\colon X^{\prime}\rightarrow S^{\prime}
, where
S^{\prime}
is Noetherian, and a morphism
g\colon S\rightarrow S^{\prime}
such that the following diagram is a pullback diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S^{\prime}\hbox to0.0pt{.\hss}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime}}

Also, by the lemma the Hodge filtration associated to
f^{\prime}
is strong. Now if the Hodge–de Rham spectral sequence associated to
f^{\prime}
degenerates and the
R^{i}f^{\prime}_{*}{}^{j}_{X^{\prime}/S^{\prime}}
are finite locally free, by the compatibility of the spectral sequence with base change, we have that the Hodge–de Rham spectral sequence associated to
f
degenerates and the
R^{i}f_{*}{}^{j}_{X/S}
are finite locally free. In particular, we may assume that
S
is Noetherian and affine. But this case was already treated in Proposition
3.71
.
∎

We conclude this section by defining the substacks corresponding to a function
\tau\colon\mathbb{Z}\rightarrow\mathbb{N}_{0}^{\mathbb{Z}}
. Again, we could do this by right Kan extension, but as before, there is also an
ad hoc
definition that agrees with the one given by right Kan extension.

The following definitions are globalizations of the definitions given in Section
3.3
.

Definition 3.75
.

Let
S
be a derived scheme, and let
{\mathcal{F}}\in\textup{Perf}(S)
. We define the function

\displaystyle\beta_{\mathcal{F}}\colon S_{\textup{cl}}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\mathbb{N}_{0}^{\mathbb{Z}}

\displaystyle s

\displaystyle\longmapsto(\dim_{\kappa(s)}\pi_{i}(\textup{cl}^{*}{\mathcal{F}}\otimes^{L}_{{\mathcal{O}}_{S_{\textup{cl}}}}\kappa(s)))_{i\in\mathbb{Z}},

where
\textup{cl}\colon S_{\textup{cl}}\rightarrow S
is the natural morphism.

This function is locally upper semi-continuous in the sense that for every
s\in S_{\textup{cl}}
, there is a neighbourhood
U_{s}
such that for any family
(k_{i})_{i}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}{Z}
, the set
\beta_{{\mathcal{F}}|U_{s}}^{-1}(([0,k_{i}])_{i})
is open (see
[
Sta24
, 0BDI]
).

Remark 3.76
.

Note that in Definition
3.75
, we implicitly assume that
\textup{cl}^{\ast}{\mathcal{F}}
is a perfect complex of quasi-coherent
{\mathcal{O}}_{S_{\textup{cl}}}
-module. This makes sense as
\textup{cl}^{*}{\mathcal{F}}
is in
{\mathcal{D}}_{\textup{qc}}(S_{\textup{cl}})
by Proposition
2.44
and is by definition perfect.

Definition and Remark 3.77
.

Let
S
be a derived scheme. Let
\underline{F}\coloneqq(C^{\bullet},D_{\bullet},\phi,\varphi_{\bullet})
be a derived
F
-zip over
S
. Consider the function

\displaystyle\beta_{\underline{F}}\colon s\longmapsto(k\longmapsto\beta_{\gr^{k}C}(s))

from
S_{\textup{cl}}
to functions
\mathbb{Z}\rightarrow\mathbb{N}_{0}{Z}
.

(1)

The function
\beta_{\underline{F}}
is called the
type of the derived
F
-zip
\underline{F}
.

(2)

Let
\tau\colon\mathbb{Z}\rightarrow\mathbb{N}_{0}{Z}
be a function with finite support. We say that
\underline{F}
has type at most
\tau
if for all
s\in S_{\textup{cl}}
, we have
\beta_{\underline{F}}(s)\leq\tau
(again, the relation is given pointwise as functions
\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Z}\rightarrow\mathbb{N}_{0}
).

Further, for any
s\in S_{\textup{cl}}
, there exist a quasi-compact open (resp. locally closed) neighbourhood
U_{s}
of
s
and a function
\tau\colon\mathbb{Z}\rightarrow\mathbb{N}_{0}^{\mathbb{Z}}
with finite support, such that
\beta_{\underline{F}|U_{s}}\leq\tau
in the sense above (resp.
\beta_{\underline{F}|U_{s}}
is constant and equal to
\tau
) (this follows from Lemma
2.52
).

Definition and Remark 3.78
.

Let
S
be a derived scheme. Let
\underline{F}\coloneqq(C^{\bullet},D_{\bullet},\phi,\varphi_{\bullet})
be a derived
F
-zip over
S
. Let us look at the function

\displaystyle\chi_{k}(\underline{F})\colon S_{\textup{cl}}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\mathbb{Z}

\displaystyle s

\displaystyle\longmapsto\chi(\textup{cl}^{*}\gr^{k}C\otimes_{{\mathcal{O}}_{S_{\textup{cl}}}}\kappa(s)),

where
\textup{cl}\colon S_{\textup{cl}}\rightarrow S
is the natural morphism.
This is a locally constant function (see
[
Sta24
, 0B9T]
). Since the filtrations on derived
F
-zips are locally bounded, we know that the function
\chi_{\underline{F}}\colon s\mapsto(k\mapsto\chi_{k}(\underline{F})(s))
is also locally constant as a map from
S_{\textup{cl}}
to functions
\mathbb{Z}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}
with finite support. We call
\chi_{\underline{F}}
the
Euler characteristic
of
\underline{F}
.

If
\tau\colon\mathbb{Z}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}
is a function with finite support, we say that
\underline{F}
has Euler characteristic
\tau
if
\chi_{\underline{F}}
is constant with value
\tau
.

Definition 3.79
.

Let
S
be a derived scheme.

(1)

Let
M\in\textup{Perf}(S)
be a perfect module over
S
. Fix a map
r\colon\mathbb{Z}\rightarrow\mathbb{N}_{0}
. We call
M

homotopy finite locally free of rank
r
if for each scheme
f\colon X\rightarrow S_{\textup{cl}}
, the
{\mathcal{O}}_{X}
-module
\pi_{i}(f^{*}\textup{cl}^{*}M)
is finite locally free of rank
r_{i}
, where we say that it is finite locally free of rank
0
if it is isomorphic to
0
.

(2)

Let
\underline{F}\coloneqq(C^{\bullet},D_{\bullet},\varphi_{\bullet})
be a derived
F
-zip over
S
and
\tau\colon\mathbb{Z}\rightarrow\mathbb{N}_{0}^{\mathbb{Z}}
be a function with finite support. We say that
\underline{F}
is
homotopy finite locally free of type
\tau
if for all
i\in\mathbb{Z}
, we have that
\gr^{i}C
is homotopy finite locally free of rank
\tau(i)
.

Definition 3.80
.

Let
\tau\colon\mathbb{Z}\rightarrow\mathbb{N}_{0}{Z}
be a function with finite support, and let
S
be a derived
R
-scheme. We define
\FZip_{\infty,R}^{\leq\tau}(S)

(
resp.
\FZip_{\infty,R}^{\tau}(S)
,
\FZip_{\infty,R}^{\chi=\tau}(S))
as the full subcategory of derived
F
-zips over
S
of type at most
\tau

(
resp. homotopy finite locally free of type
\tau
, of Euler characteristic
\tau)
.

Lemma 3.81
.

Let
\tau\colon\mathbb{Z}\rightarrow\mathbb{N}_{0}{Z}
be a function with finite support.
Let
\mathbb{R}\FZip^{\leq\tau}_{\infty,R}

(
resp.
\mathbb{R}\FZip_{\infty,R}^{\tau}
,
\mathbb{R}\FZip_{\infty,R}^{\chi=\tau})
be the right Kan extension of
\FZip^{\leq\tau}_{\infty,R}

(
resp.
\FZip_{\infty,R}^{\tau}
,
\FZip_{\infty,R}^{\chi=\tau})
along the Yoneda embedding
\textup{AR}_{R}\hookrightarrow{\mathcal{P}}(\textup{AR}_{R}^{\op})^{\op}
. Then for any derived scheme
S
over
R
, the natural morphism induced by base change

\displaystyle\FZip^{\leq\tau}_{\infty,R}(S)\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}\FZip^{\leq\tau}_{\infty,R}(S)

\displaystyle\textup{ (resp.\ }\FZip^{\tau}_{\infty,R}(S)\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}\FZip^{\tau}_{\infty,R}(S),\ \FZip^{\chi=\tau}_{\infty,R}(S)\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}\FZip^{\chi=\tau}_{\infty,R}(S)\textup{)}

is an equivalence.

Proof.

The proof is completely analogous to that of Lemma
3.65
. We only need to verify that the properties “has type
\leq\tau
”, “is homotopy finite locally free of type
\tau
” and “has Euler characteristic
\tau
” can be checked on an affine open cover of
S
, but this is clear.
∎

Definition and Remark 3.82
.

Let
\tau\colon\mathbb{Z}\rightarrow\mathbb{N}_{0}{Z}
be a function with finite support, and let
S
be a derived
R
-scheme. We define

\FZip_{R}^{\leq\tau}\colon{\mathcal{P}}(\textup{AR}_{R}^{\op})^{\op}\longrightarrow\mathbb{S}

(resp.
\FZip_{R}^{\tau}
,
\FZip_{R}^{\chi=\tau}
) as the right Kan extension of
\FZip_{R}^{\leq\tau}
(resp.
\FZip_{R}^{\tau}
,
\FZip_{R}^{\chi=\tau}
) along the Yoneda embedding
\textup{AR}_{R}\hookrightarrow{\mathcal{P}}(\textup{AR}_{R}^{\op})^{\op}
.

By Remark
2.41
, these define fpqc sheaves and define subsheaves of
\FZip_{R}
.

3.6. Perfect complexes on the pinched projective space

In this subsection, we want to understand the perfect complexes on the pinched projective space,
i.e.
the
\infty
-category
\QQCoh_{\perf}({\mathfrak X}_{S})
for a scheme
S
in characteristic
p>0
. (See the appendix for the notation). We show in the appendix that the vector bundles on
{\mathfrak X}
are precisely the
F
-zips. In this section, we will see a similar result for derived
F
-zips; namely, we will show that
\QQCoh_{\perf}({\mathfrak X}_{S})\simeq\FZip_{\infty,R}(S)
.

As explained in
[
HL13
, Proposition 4.1.1]
, quasi-coherent sheaves on
[\mathbb{A}_{S}^{1}/\Gm_{,S}]
are the same as
\mathbb{Z}
-indexed diagrams of quasi-coherent
{\mathcal{O}}_{S}
-modules, so a chain of morphisms of
{\mathcal{O}}_{S}
-modules

\cdots\longrightarrow{\mathcal{F}}_{i}\longrightarrow{\mathcal{F}}_{i+1}\longrightarrow{\mathcal{F}}_{i+2}\longrightarrow\cdots

(for vector bundles, we showed the computation behind it in Theorem
A.2
). Equivalently, the category of quasi-coherent sheaves on
[\mathbb{A}_{S}^{1}/\Gm_{,S}]
is given by the category of graded
{\mathcal{O}}_{S}
-modules together with an endomorphism of degree
1
(this endomorphism is induced by multiplication with
X
).
This gives a description of the category of chain complexes and so

D_{\textup{qc}}\left(\left[\mathbb{A}_{S}^{1}/\Gm_{,S}\right]\right)\simeq\Fun(\mathbb{Z},D_{\textup{qc}}(S)).

Now let us endow the abelian category of chain complexes of quasi-coherent modules over
[\mathbb{A}_{S}^{1}/\Gm_{,S}]
with the usual model structure and
\Fun(\mathbb{Z},\Ch(\QQCoh(S)))
with the pointwise model structure. The natural identification of the categories explained above induces a Quillen equivalence and therefore an equivalence of
\infty
-categories

{\mathcal{D}}_{\textup{qc}}\left(\left[\mathbb{A}_{S}^{1}/\Gm_{,S}\right]\right)\simeq\Fun(\mathbb{Z},{\mathcal{D}}_{\textup{qc}}(S)).

In fact, this equivalence can be upgraded naturally to a symmetric monoidal equivalence.

We will compute
\QQCoh_{\perf}({\mathfrak X}_{S})
via descent. For this, we want to understand the pullback of perfect complexes along
[\{0\}/\Gm_{,\mathbb{F}_{p}}]\rightarrow[\mathbb{A}^{1}_{S}/\Gm_{,\mathbb{F}_{p}}]
. We will need the following proposition to ease computation.

Lemma 3.83
.

Let
S
be a scheme. Further, let
G
be a group scheme over
S
, and let
X
and
Z
be
S
-schemes with a smooth
G
-action denoted by
a_{X}
and
a_{Z}
, respectively.
Let
i\colon Z\hookrightarrow X
be a
G
-equivariant closed immersion such that the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G\times_{S}Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G\times_{S}X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\id\times i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a_{Z}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}

is Cartesian. Then the restriction functor
i_{*}\colon{\mathcal{D}}_{\textup{qc}}(\left[Z/G\right])\rightarrow{\mathcal{D}}_{\textup{qc}}(\left[X/G\right])
is conservative.

Proof.

Using the Barr resolution of
\left[Z/G\right]
and
\left[X/G\right]
, we get a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G\times_{S}Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G\times_{S}X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\hbox to0.0pt{,\hss}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a_{Z}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\id\times i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}

where the vertical arrows are all closed immersion. The derived pushforward along the vertical arrows is therefore conservative (see
[
Sta24
, 08I8]
). As the derived
\infty
-category with quasi-coherent cohomology satisfies fpqc descent (see Remark
2.43
), this concludes the proof.
∎

Proposition 3.84
.

Let
\Gm_{,S}
act on
\mathbb{A}^{1}_{S}
by multiplication of degree
1

(
resp.
-1)
and trivially on the closed subscheme
\{0\}\subseteq\mathbb{A}^{1}_{S}
. We denote by
f\colon[\{0\}/\Gm_{,S}]\rightarrow[\mathbb{A}_{S}^{1}/\Gm_{,S}]
the naturally induced morphism. Further, let
M\in{\mathcal{D}}_{\textup{qc}}([\mathbb{A}_{S}^{1}/\Gm_{,S}])
. Then we have

Lf^{*}M\simeq\bigoplusop\displaylimits_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}\gr^{i}M,

where we consider
M
as an element of
\Fun(\mathbb{Z},{\mathcal{D}}_{\textup{qc}}(S))
.

Proof.

We first claim that it is enough to show that after restricting to
{\mathcal{D}}_{\textup{qc}}([\mathbb{A}_{S}^{1}/\Gm_{,S}])
, we have
Lf^{*}M\simeq\bigoplusop\displaylimits_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}\gr^{i}M
. In particular, to compute
Lf^{*}
, we may resolve
{\mathcal{O}}_{S}
as a K-flat complex in
{\mathcal{D}}_{\textup{qc}}([\mathbb{A}_{S}^{1}/\Gm_{,S}])
, which is straightforward as we are going to see.

Indeed, let
F\colon{\mathcal{D}}_{\textup{qc}}([\mathbb{A}_{S}^{1}/\Gm_{,S}])\rightarrow{\mathcal{D}}_{\textup{qc}}([\{0\}/\Gm_{,S}])
denote the graded functor
M\mapsto\bigoplusop\displaylimits_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}\gr^{i}M
. Assume that
f_{*}Lf^{*}M\simeq f_{*}F(M)
. Then by adjunction, we get a morphism
\alpha\colon Lf^{*}M\rightarrow F(M)
in
{\mathcal{D}}_{\textup{qc}}([\{0\}/\Gm_{,S}])
induced by the identity on
Lf^{*}M
. But
\alpha
is an equivalence by Lemma
3.83
(the smoothness of the
\Gm_{,S}
-action on
\mathbb{A}^{1}_{S}
follows from the proof of Lemma
A.1
), concluding the reduction step.

We will give a proof in the case where
\Gm_{,S}
acts by multiplication of degree
1
. The degree
-1
case is completely analogous; we will note the places where the proof changes.

Important for us is that, as explained above, a quasi-coherent
\Gm_{,S}
-equivariant
{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbb{A}^{1}_{S}}
-module
{\mathcal{F}}
is equivalently a graded
{\mathcal{O}}_{S}
-module
{\mathcal{F}}=\bigoplusop\displaylimits_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}{\mathcal{F}}^{i}
together with an endomorphism
{\mathcal{F}}\rightarrow{\mathcal{F}}
of degree
1
(resp.
-1
) that is induced by multiplication with
X
.

The category of quasi-coherent modules over
[\{0\}/\Gm_{,S}]
is analogously equivalent to the category of quasi-coherent graded
{\mathcal{O}}_{S}
-modules. As
f
is equivariant, we get a pullback functor
f^{*}
from the category of cochain complexes of graded
{\mathcal{O}}_{S}
-modules with endomorphism of degree
1
(resp.
-1
) to the category of cochain complexes of graded
{\mathcal{O}}_{S}
-modules. Let us write
M
as
(M_{\bullet},\partial_{\bullet})=(\bigoplusop\displaylimits_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}M^{i}_{\bullet},\bigoplusop\displaylimits_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}\partial^{i}_{\bullet})
(a chain complex of graded
{\mathcal{O}}_{S}
-modules) together with an endomorphism
X\colon M\rightarrow M
that is induced by multiplication with
X
. The complex
f^{*}M
is given by
M\otimes_{{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbb{A}^{1}_{S}}}{\mathcal{O}}_{S}
, where we identify
{\mathcal{O}}_{S}
with
{\mathcal{O}}_{S}[X]/(X)
(which endows
{\mathcal{O}}_{S}
with a trivial grading and degree
1
(resp.
-1
) endomorphism given by
0
).

To compute
Lf^{*}M
, it is enough to find a cochain complex
P\in{\mathcal{D}}_{\textup{qc}}([\mathbb{A}_{S}^{1}/\Gm_{,S}])
with a quasi-isomorphism
P\xrightarrow{\vbox to0.0pt{\vss\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sim$}\vskip-2.0pt}}{\mathcal{O}}_{S}
in
{\mathcal{D}}_{\textup{qc}}([\mathbb{A}_{S}^{1}/\Gm_{,S}])
, such that the functor
P\otimes_{{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbb{A}^{1}_{S}}}\!\!-
in the category of cochain complexes of
\Gm_{,S}
-equivariant
{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbb{A}^{1}_{S}}
-modules is exact. Then
Lf^{*}M
is equivalent to
M\otimes_{{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbb{A}^{1}_{S}}}\!\!P
. We claim that
P
is naturally given by the Koszul complex of
{\mathcal{O}}_{S}
.

Indeed, a flat resolution of
{\mathcal{O}}_{S}
is given by the complex
P^{\bullet}
that is zero everywhere except in degrees
-1
and
0
, where it is given by the morphism

{\mathcal{O}}_{S}[X]\xrightarrow{\,\cdot X\,}{\mathcal{O}}_{S}[X].

Now
{\mathcal{O}}_{S}[X]
is endowed with the obvious grading together with an endomorphism of degree
1
(resp.
-1
) by multiplication with
X
. Then
P^{\bullet}
becomes an element of
{\mathcal{D}}_{\textup{qc}}([\mathbb{A}_{S}^{1}/\Gm_{,S}])
by shifting the grading of
P^{-1}
by
-1
(resp.
1
). Note that the functor
P^{\bullet}\otimes_{{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbb{A}^{1}_{S}}}-
is exact, and therefore we have

Lf^{*}M\simeq P^{\bullet}\otimes_{{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbb{A}^{1}_{S}}}\!\!M.

Now let us explicitly compute
Lf^{*}M
. By the definition of the tensor product of chain complexes, we deduce from the above that
M\otimes_{{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbb{A}^{1}_{S}}}\!\!P^{\bullet}
is equivalent to the complex

(M_{n+1}\oplus M_{n},\iota_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}},

where the differentials
\iota_{n}
are given by

\begin{pmatrix}\partial_{n+1}&0\\
(-1)^{n}X&\partial_{n}\end{pmatrix}.

The induced grading is given by
(M_{n+1}\oplus M_{n})^{i}=M_{n+1}^{i-1}\oplus M_{n}^{i}
(resp.
(M_{n+1}\oplus M_{n})^{i}=M_{n+1}^{i+1}\oplus M_{n}^{i}
).

Now let us analyze the graded pieces
\gr^{i}M
. As explained above, we can also consider

\cdots\longrightarrow(M^{i-1}_{\bullet},\partial^{-1}_{\bullet})\xrightarrow{\,\cdot X\,}(M^{i}_{\bullet},\partial^{i}_{\bullet})\longrightarrow\cdots

as a filtration in
{\mathcal{D}}_{\textup{qc}}(S)
. Let us calculate
\cofib((M^{i-1}_{\bullet},\partial^{i-1}_{\bullet})\xrightarrow{\cdot X}(M^{i}_{\bullet},\partial^{i}_{\bullet}))
. We can do so by calculating the cone of multiplication with
X
, which is given by

\left(M^{i-1}_{n+1}\oplus M^{i}_{n},\iota_{n}^{i}\right)_{n\in\mathbb{Z}},

where the differentials
\iota_{n}^{i}
are, up to equivalence, given by

\begin{pmatrix}\partial^{i-1}_{n+1}&0\\
(-1)^{n}X&\partial^{i}_{n}\end{pmatrix}.

(For
\cofib((M^{i+1}_{\bullet},\partial^{i-1}_{\bullet})\xrightarrow{\cdot X}(M^{i}_{\bullet},\partial^{i}_{\bullet}))
, this is analogous; one just changes the indices).

Finally, these constructions imply that
Lf^{*}M\simeq\bigoplusop\displaylimits_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}\gr^{i}M
in
{\mathcal{D}}_{\textup{qc}}([\mathbb{A}_{S}^{1}/\Gm_{,S}])
.
∎

We are finally ready to compute the perfect complexes on
{\mathfrak X}_{S}
for any scheme
S
. We will describe it as a full subcategory of

\displaystyle{\mathcal{C}}(S)\coloneqq(\Fun(\mathbb{Z}^{\op},{\mathcal{D}}_{\textup{qc}}(S))

\displaystyle\times_{\mathop{\textup{colim}},{\mathcal{D}}_{\textup{qc}}(S),\mathop{\textup{colim}}}\Fun(\mathbb{Z},{\mathcal{D}}_{\textup{qc}}(S)))

\displaystyle\times_{(\bigoplusop\displaylimits(\gr^{i})^{(1)},\bigoplusop\displaylimits\gr^{i}),{\mathcal{D}}_{\textup{qc}}(S)\times{\mathcal{D}}_{\textup{qc}}(S),\dom\times\codom}\Fun(\Delta^{1},{\mathcal{D}}_{\textup{qc}}(S)).

Theorem 3.85
.

For any scheme
S
, the
\infty
-category
\QQCoh_{\perf}({\mathfrak X}_{S})
is equivalent to the full subcategory in
{\mathcal{C}}(S)
of tuples
(C^{\bullet},D_{\bullet},\phi,\varphi)
, where

•

C^{\bullet}\in\Fun(\mathbb{Z}^{\op},{\mathcal{D}}_{\textup{qc}}(S))
is such that all
\gr^{i}C
and
\bigoplusop\displaylimits_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}\gr^{i}C
are perfect;

•

D_{\bullet}\in\Fun(\mathbb{Z},{\mathcal{D}}_{\textup{qc}}(S))
is such that all
\gr^{i}D
and
\bigoplusop\displaylimits_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}\gr^{i}D
are perfect;

•

\phi\colon\mathop{\textup{colim}}_{\mathbb{Z}^{\op}}C^{\bullet}\xrightarrow{\vbox to0.0pt{\vss\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sim$}\vskip-2.0pt}}\mathop{\textup{colim}}_{\mathbb{Z}}D^{\bullet}
is an equivalence; and

•

\varphi\colon\bigoplusop\displaylimits_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}\gr^{i}C^{(1)}\xrightarrow{\vbox to0.0pt{\vss\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sim$}\vskip-2.0pt}}\bigoplusop\displaylimits_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}\gr^{i}D
is an equivalence.

Proof.

By Proposition
2.39
, we can use the Barr resolution of
{\mathfrak X}_{S}
to see that

(3.7)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\QQCoh_{\perf}({\mathfrak X}_{S})\simeq\lim\big(\QQCoh_{\perf}(X_{S})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\QQCoh_{\perf}\left(X_{S}\times_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}\Gm_{,\mathbb{F}_{p}}\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots\big)\hbox to0.0pt{.\hss}}

We can again use Proposition
2.39
to see that we have a limit diagram of the form

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\QQCoh_{\perf}(X_{S})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\QQCoh_{\perf}\left(\mathbb{P}_{S}^{1}\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\QQCoh_{\perf}(\{\infty\})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\QQCoh_{\perf}(\{\infty\})\times\QQCoh_{\perf}(\{0\})\hbox to0.0pt{.\hss}}

Since the
\Gm_{,\mathbb{F}_{p}}
-action on
X_{S}
is induced by the pushout of the
\Gm_{,\mathbb{F}_{p}}
-actions on
\mathbb{P}^{1}_{S}
,
\{\infty\}
and
\{\infty\}\amalg\{0\}
(see (
A.1
)), we see that an object
X\in\QQCoh_{\perf}({\mathfrak X}_{S})
corresponds to a tuple
(M,N,\varphi)
, where

•

M\in\QQCoh_{\perf}([\mathbb{P}^{1}_{S}/\Gm_{,\mathbb{F}_{p}}])
,

•

N\in\QQCoh_{\perf}([\{\infty\}/\Gm_{,\mathbb{F}_{p}}])
, and

•

\varphi
is an equivalence of the images of
M
and
N
in

\QQCoh_{\perf}\left(\left[\{\infty\}/\Gm_{,\mathbb{F}_{p}}\right]\right)\times\QQCoh_{\perf}\left(\left[\{0\}/\Gm_{,\mathbb{F}_{p}}\right]\right).

Using the standard cover of
\mathbb{P}^{1}_{S}
by affine lines and the discussion at the beginning of this subsection, we see that
M\in\QQCoh_{\perf}([\mathbb{P}^{1}_{S}/\Gm_{,\mathbb{F}_{p}}])
is equivalently given by a tuple
(C^{\bullet},D_{\bullet},\phi)
, where
C^{\bullet}\in\Fun(\mathbb{Z}^{\op},{\mathcal{D}}_{\textup{qc}}(S))
is perfect,
D_{\bullet}\in\Fun(\mathbb{Z},{\mathcal{D}}_{\textup{qc}}(S))
is perfect and
\phi\colon\mathop{\textup{colim}}_{\mathbb{Z}^{\op}}C^{\bullet}\xrightarrow{\vbox to0.0pt{\vss\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sim$}\vskip-2.0pt}}\mathop{\textup{colim}}_{\mathbb{Z}}D^{\bullet}
is an equivalence.
(25)
(25)

(25)

By construction,
\mathbb{P}^{1}_{S}
is the pushout of the maps
\Spec({\mathcal{O}}_{S}[X,X^{-1}])\xrightarrow{x\mapsto x^{-1}}\Spec({\mathcal{O}}_{S}[X])
and
\Spec({\mathcal{O}}_{S}[X,X^{-1}])\xrightarrow{x\mapsto x}\Spec({\mathcal{O}}_{S}[X])
,
where the maps are given on
T
-valued points. So again, the description of
\QQCoh_{\perf}(\mathbb{P}^{1}_{S})
follows from Proposition
2.39
and the fact that the derived pullback along open immersions is given by the usual pullback.

Further (as explained in the proof of Proposition
3.84
),
\QQCoh_{\perf}([\{\infty\}/\Gm_{,\mathbb{F}_{p}}])
consists of perfect chain complexes of graded
{\mathcal{O}}_{S}
-modules. Also, we have seen in Proposition
3.84
that the image of
(C^{\bullet},D_{\bullet})
in the product
\QQCoh_{\perf}([\{\infty\}/\Gm_{,\mathbb{F}_{p}}])\times\QQCoh_{\perf}([\{0\}/\Gm_{,\mathbb{F}_{p}}])
is equivalent to
\left(\bigoplusop\displaylimits_{i}\gr^{i}C,\bigoplusop\displaylimits_{i}\gr^{i}D\right)
.
Lastly, we want to note that by
[
GP18
, Proposition 2.45]
, an element in
\Fun(\mathbb{Z}^{\op},{\mathcal{D}}_{\textup{qc}}(S))
(resp.
\Fun(\mathbb{Z},{\mathcal{D}}_{\textup{qc}}(S))
) is perfect if and only if each graded piece is perfect.

Combining all of this, we get the desired description of
\QQCoh_{\perf}({\mathfrak X}_{S})
as a full subcategory of
{\mathcal{C}}(S)
.
∎

It is clear by construction that
\FZip(S)
is a full subcategory of
\QQCoh_{\perf}({\mathfrak X}_{S})
. But let us show that we have an equivalence. This will follow immediately if we can show that the filtrations associated to an element in
\QQCoh_{\perf}({\mathfrak X}_{S})
are locally bounded.

Lemma 3.86
.

Let
S
be a scheme, and let
F\in\Fun(\mathbb{Z},{\mathcal{D}}_{\textup{qc}}(S))
be an ascending filtration such that
\gr^{i}F
and
\bigoplusop\displaylimits_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}\gr^{i}F
are perfect
{\mathcal{O}}_{S}
-modules. Then
F
is locally bounded and perfect.

The assertion stays true if we replace
\mathbb{Z}
with
\mathbb{Z}^{\op}
.

Proof.

As this is a local question, we may assume that
S=\Spec(A)
is affine. Fiberwise, the question is clear since a perfect complex over a field is quasi-isomorphic to a finite direct sum of finite-dimensional vector spaces sitting in one degree. For every point
s\in S
, we can find an open neighbourhood
U_{s}
around
s
such that only finitely many
\gr^{i}F
are non-zero (see
[
Sta24
, 0BCD]
). As
S
is quasi-compact, we deduce the lemma.
(26)
(26)

(26)

Inductively, any bounded filtration with perfect graded pieces is perfect.

∎

Corollary 3.87
.

Let
R
be an
\mathbb{F}_{p}
-algebra and
S
an
R
-scheme. Then we have

\FZip_{\infty,R}(S)\simeq\QQCoh_{\perf}({\mathfrak X}_{S}).

Proof.

This follows immediately by combining Theorem
3.85
, Lemma
3.86
and that finite direct sums in
{\mathcal{D}}_{\textup{qc}}(S)
are the same as finite products as
{\mathcal{D}}_{\textup{qc}}(S)
is stable.
(27)
(27)

(27)

The equivalence between finite direct sums and products shows that for an element
(C^{\bullet},D_{\bullet},\phi,\varphi)\in\QQCoh_{\perf}({\mathfrak X}_{S})
, the equivalence
\varphi\colon\bigoplusop\displaylimits_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}\gr^{i}C^{(1)}\xrightarrow{\vbox to0.0pt{\vss\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sim$}\vskip-2.0pt}}\bigoplusop\displaylimits_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}\gr^{i}D
is equivalently given by equivalences
\varphi_{i}\colon\gr^{i}C^{(1)}\xrightarrow{\vbox to0.0pt{\vss\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sim$}\vskip-2.0pt}}\gr^{i}D
.

∎

4. Connection to classical theory

Again, in the following
R
will be an
\mathbb{F}_{p}
-algebra.

4.1. Derived
\boldsymbol{F}
-zips with degenerating spectral sequences

In Lemma
3.54
, we showed that classical
F
-zips can be included in the theory of derived
F
-zips. But what if the homotopy groups associated to the graded pieces of a derived
F
-zip are finite locally free and the associated spectral sequences degenerate (see Definition
4.1
)? Then we would expect that we have a functor
\pi_{n}\colon{\mathcal{X}}\rightarrow\textup{cl}\FZip
, where
{\mathcal{X}}
is a suitable substack of
t_{0}\FZip
, given by sending the underlying module to its
n^{\mathrm{th}}
homotopy group and looking at the associated filtrations.

In fact, we will show that there is even more. For a smooth proper scheme morphism with degenerating Hodge–de Rham spectral sequence and finite locally free cohomologies, we get an
F
-zip. Important here is that the graded pieces and de Rham cohomology are finite locally free. For filtrations in our sense, we also get spectral sequences;
i.e.
for some
R
-algebra
A
and bounded perfect filtrations
C^{\bullet}\in\Fun(\mathbb{Z}^{\op},{\mathcal{D}}(A))
and
D_{\bullet}\in\Fun(\mathbb{Z},{\mathcal{D}}(A))
, we have spectral sequences

E_{1}^{p,q}=\pi_{p+q}(\gr^{p}C)\Longrightarrow\pi_{p+q}\mathop{\textup{colim}}_{\mathbb{Z}^{\op}}C^{\bullet}\quad\textup{and}\quad E_{1}^{p,q}=\pi_{p+q}(\gr^{p}D)\Longrightarrow\pi_{p+q}\mathop{\textup{colim}}_{\mathbb{Z}}D_{\bullet}

(see
[
Lur17
, Proposition 1.2.2.14]
). If we assume that the
\pi_{p+q}(\gr^{p}C)
are finite projective and that the above spectral sequences are degenerate, we can associate, for any
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, a classical
F
-zip to a derived
F
-zip
\underline{F}\coloneqq(C^{\bullet},D_{\bullet},\phi,\varphi_{\bullet})
via

\underline{F}\mapsto\pi_{n}\underline{F}\coloneqq\left(\pi_{n}\left(\mathop{\textup{colim}}_{\mathbb{Z}^{\op}}C^{\bullet}\right),\widetilde{C}^{\bullet},\widetilde{D}_{\bullet},\pi_{n}\varphi_{\bullet}\right),

where
\widetilde{C}^{\bullet}
, resp.
\widetilde{D}_{\bullet}
, is the filtration associated to the spectral sequences induced by
C^{\bullet}
, resp.
D_{\bullet}
. Let us verify that
(\pi_{n}(\mathop{\textup{colim}}_{\mathbb{Z}^{\op}}C^{\bullet}),\widetilde{C}^{\bullet},\widetilde{D}_{\bullet},\pi_{n}\varphi_{\bullet})
is a classical
F
-zip.

For convenience, let us set
M\coloneqq\pi_{n}(\mathop{\textup{colim}}_{\mathbb{Z}^{\op}}C^{\bullet})
. By definition, we also have
\pi_{n}\mathop{\textup{colim}}_{\mathbb{Z}}{D}\cong M
. First of all, note that both
\widetilde{C}^{\bullet}
and
\widetilde{D}_{\bullet}
are finite and that by the degeneracy of the spectral sequences, their graded pieces are equivalent to

\gr^{i}_{\widetilde{C}}M=\pi_{n}(\gr^{i}C),\quad\gr^{i}_{\widetilde{D}}M=\pi_{n}(\gr^{i}D).

By homotopy finite projectiveness, all graded pieces of
\widetilde{C}^{\bullet}
and
\widetilde{D}_{\bullet}
are finite projective.
(28)
(28)

(28)

Note that the homotopy finite projectiveness of
\pi_{n}\gr^{i}C
implies the compatibility with base change (along Frobenius), by Lemma
3.40
(
3
), and so
\pi_{n}\gr^{i}D\cong\pi_{n}(\gr^{i}C^{(1)})\cong\pi_{n}(\gr^{i}C)^{(1)}
is finite projective.
Since the filtrations are bounded, we see that the pieces of the filtrations are also finite projective, and thus
M
also is. The only thing left to see is that
\pi_{n}\varphi_{i}
induce isomorphisms
(\gr^{i}_{\widetilde{C}}M)^{(1)}\xrightarrow{\vbox to0.0pt{\vss\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sim$}\vskip-2.0pt}}\gr^{i}_{\widetilde{D}}M
. But this again follows from the degeneracy of the spectral sequences (resp. the description above induced by the degeneracy) and the fact that homotopy finite projectiveness implies compatibility with base change (along Frobenius) by Lemma
3.40
(
3
).

Further, if the derived
F
-zip
\underline{F}
is homotopy finite projective of some type
\tau
, then
\pi_{n}\underline{F}
has type
\tau_{n}\colon k\mapsto\tau(k)_{n}
.

Moreover, using the arguments in the proof of Theorem
3.74
, we see that a derived
F
-zip homotopy finite projective of some type with degenerating spectral sequences associated to the filtrations (as above) is automatically strong. Let us make everything we said above more precise.

Definition 4.1
.

Let
A
be an
R
-algebra. A derived
F
-zip
(C^{\bullet},D_{\bullet},\phi,\varphi_{\bullet})
is called
degenerate
if the spectral sequences

E_{1}^{p,q}=\pi_{p+q}(\gr^{p}C)\Longrightarrow\pi_{p+q}\mathop{\textup{colim}}_{\mathbb{Z}^{\op}}C^{\bullet}\quad\textup{and}\quad E_{1}^{p,q}=\pi_{p+q}(\gr^{p}D)\Longrightarrow\pi_{p+q}\mathop{\textup{colim}}_{\mathbb{Z}}D_{\bullet}

associated to the filtrations (see
[
Lur17
, Proposition 1.2.2.14]
) degenerate.

Lemma 4.2
.

Let
A
be an
R
-algebra, and let
\tau\colon\mathbb{Z}\rightarrow\mathbb{N}_{0}^{\mathbb{Z}}
be a function with finite support. If a derived
F
-zip
\underline{F}
over
A
is homotopy finite projective of type
\tau
and degenerate, then
\underline{F}
is strong.

Proof.

This follows from Proposition
3.73
.
∎

Proposition 4.3
.

Let
A
be an
R
-algebra, and let
\tau\colon\mathbb{Z}\rightarrow\mathbb{N}_{0}^{\mathbb{Z}}
be a function with finite support. Further, let
{\mathcal{X}}^{\tau}_{\infty,R}(A)\subseteq\FZip_{\infty,R}^{\tau}(A)
denote the full subcategory of those derived
F
-zips that are homotopy finite projective of type
\tau
and degenerate. Then
A\mapsto{\mathcal{X}}^{\tau}_{\infty,R}(A)
defines a hypercomplete sheaf for the fpqc topology.

Moreover, let
{\mathcal{X}}_{R}^{\tau}
denote the associated derived stack. Then the inclusion

i\colon{\mathcal{X}}_{R}^{\tau}\xhookrightarrow{\hphantom{aaa}}t_{0}\FZip_{R}^{\tau}

is a closed immersion.

Proof.

Analogously to the proof of Proposition
3.49
, it suffices to check that the transition maps of the spectral sequences are zero if and only if they are zero fpqc locally, but as these are maps between discrete modules, we see that this is certainly an fpqc local property.

Let
\Spec(A)\rightarrow t_{0}\FZip_{R}^{\tau}
be given by a derived
F
-Zip
\underline{F}
that is homotopy finite projective of type
\tau
. This in particular implies that the formation of the homologies of the graded pieces commutes with arbitrary base change, by Lemma
3.40
(
3
). Now a morphism
f\colon\Spec(T)\rightarrow\Spec(A)
factors through
{\mathcal{X}}_{R}^{\tau}\times_{t_{0}\FZip_{R}^{\tau}}\Spec(A)
if and only if the spectral sequences associated to the filtrations of
f^{*}\underline{F}
degenerate. Again, this is equivalent to the differentials of the spectral sequences being zero. By the commutativity of the homologies with base change and the fact that being zero for a morphism of finite projective modules is a closed property (see
[
GW10
, Proposition 8.4(2)]
), we see that
i
is in fact a closed immersion.
∎

Remark 4.4
.

Let
A
be an
R
-algebra.
Let
\tau\colon\mathbb{Z}\rightarrow\mathbb{N}_{0}^{\mathbb{Z}}
be a function with finite support, and let
n\in\mathbb{Z}
. As explained at the beginning of this section, we get a map of derived stacks

\pi_{n}\colon{\mathcal{X}}_{R}^{\tau}\longrightarrow\textup{cl}\FZip_{R}^{\tau_{n}}

that is induced by
\underline{F}\mapsto\pi_{n}\underline{F}
, where
\tau_{n}\colon\mathbb{Z}\rightarrow\mathbb{N}_{0}
is given by the function
k\mapsto\tau(k)_{n}
. Also, by Lemma
4.2
, we see that the inclusion
{\mathcal{X}}_{R}^{\tau}\hookrightarrow t_{0}\FZip_{R}^{\tau}
factors through the open derived substack
t_{0}\sFZip_{R}^{\tau}
.

We can also include
\textup{cl}\FZip_{R}^{\tau_{n}}
in
{\mathcal{X}}_{R}^{\tau}
by considering the functor

(-)[n]\colon\underline{M}\coloneqq(M,C^{\bullet},D_{\bullet},\varphi_{\bullet})\longmapsto\underline{M}[n]\coloneqq(C_{n}^{\bullet},D^{n}_{\bullet},\id^{n}_{M},\varphi^{n}_{\bullet}),

where
C_{n}^{k}\coloneqq C^{k}[n]
,
D^{n}_{k}\coloneqq D_{k}[n]
,
\id_{M}^{n}\coloneqq\id_{M}[n]
and
\varphi^{n}_{k}\coloneqq\varphi_{k}[n]
(this is just the
n
-shift of
\underline{M}[0]
in
\FZip_{R}(A)
). Thus,
\pi_{n}
defines a section of
(-)[n]
.

We see that the morphism given by

\prodop\displaylimits_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\textup{cl}\FZip_{R}^{\tau_{n}}\xhookrightarrow{\hphantom{aaa}}{\mathcal{X}}_{R}^{\tau},\quad(\underline{M}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\longmapsto\bigoplusop\displaylimits_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\underline{M}_{n}[n]

is a monomorphism, as it is a product of monomorphisms (note that
\tau
has finite support, and so the morphism above is induced by the termwise inclusions).

Lemma 4.5
.

Let
\tau\colon\mathbb{Z}\rightarrow\mathbb{N}_{0}^{\mathbb{Z}}
be a function with finite support and finitely many values. Then the monomorphism
\prodop\displaylimits_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\textup{cl}\FZip_{R}^{\tau_{n}}\hookrightarrow{\mathcal{X}}_{R}^{\tau}
defined in Remark
4.4
is an equivalence of derived stacks.

Proof.

We have to show that the map
\prodop\displaylimits_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\textup{cl}\FZip_{R}^{\tau_{n}}\hookrightarrow{\mathcal{X}}_{R}^{\tau}
is an effective epimorphism.

It is enough to show that for an
R
-algebra
A
, every
\underline{F}\in{\mathcal{X}}^{\tau}_{R}(A)
is equivalent to
\bigoplusop\displaylimits_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\underline{M}_{n}[n]
for some
F
-zips
\underline{M}_{n}\in\textup{cl}\FZip^{\tau_{n}}_{R}(A)
.

Let us set
\underline{F}\simeq(C^{\bullet},D_{\bullet},\varphi_{\bullet})
(see Remark
3.17
). We can assume that for every
k\in\mathbb{Z}
, we have
C^{k}\simeq\bigoplusop\displaylimits_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}C^{k}_{n}[n]
and
D_{k}\simeq\bigoplusop\displaylimits_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}D_{k}^{n}[n]
, by Lemma
3.69
. Let
\pi_{n}\underline{F}=(M,\widetilde{C}^{\bullet},\widetilde{D}_{\bullet},\pi_{n}\varphi_{\bullet})
, as in the beginning of this section. By the strongness of
\underline{F}
and the constructions of
\widetilde{C}^{\bullet}
and
\widetilde{D}_{\bullet}
, we see that
\widetilde{C}^{k}=C^{k}_{n}
and
\widetilde{D}_{k}=D_{k}^{n}
. Thus, we immediately see that
\underline{F}\simeq\bigoplusop\displaylimits_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\pi_{n}\underline{F}[n]
.
∎

Lemma 4.6
.

Let us fix some
n\in\mathbb{Z}
. Further, let
\sigma\colon\mathbb{Z}\rightarrow\mathbb{N}_{0}
be a function with finite support and
\tau^{\sigma}_{n}\colon\mathbb{Z}\rightarrow\mathbb{N}_{0}^{\mathbb{Z}}
be given by
k\mapsto\tau^{\sigma}_{n}(k)_{n}=\sigma(k)
and
k\mapsto\tau^{\sigma}_{n}(k)_{m}=0
for
m\neq n
. Then the inclusion
{\mathcal{X}}_{R}^{\sigma}\hookrightarrow t_{0}\FZip_{R}^{\sigma}
is quasi-compact open.

Proof.

By Lemma
4.5
, we have an equivalence
{\mathcal{X}}_{R}^{\sigma}\simeq\textup{cl}\FZip_{R}^{\tau_{n}^{\sigma}}
which is quasi-compact open and closed in
t_{0}\FZip^{\sigma}_{R}
by Lemma
3.54
(note that Lemma
3.54
assumes
n=0
, but the proof for arbitrary
n
works similarly).
∎

The results in this section show us that for morphisms with degenerating Hodge–de Rham spectral sequence, there is no new information coming from the theory of derived
F
-zips. This, for example, will also show that in the case of abelian schemes, where the
F
-zips associated to its de Rham cohomology are already determined by its
H^{1}_{\dR}
, the derived
F
-zip is also determined by
H^{1}_{\dR}
(see Section
4.2.1
).

In the next section, we want to discuss some classical examples, like curves and K3-surfaces. Analogously to the abelian scheme case, we can use Lemma
4.5
to determine the associated derived
F
-zips by their classical counterparts. We will not do this, as this is completely analogous, but will focus on derived
F
-zips with type given by the types associated to proper smooth curves and K3-surfaces. As the type
\underline{Rf_{*}{}_{X/S}}
in these cases will have a certain form, we will see that any strong derived
F
-zip with the same type is equivalent to a derived
F
-zip coming from a classical one.

4.2. Classical examples

We want to look at derived
F
-zips associated to abelian schemes, proper smooth curves and K3-surfaces, and explicitly show that we do not get anything new from the theory of derived
F
-zips.

4.2.1. Abelian schemes

Let
X\rightarrow S
be an abelian scheme of relative dimension
n
. A classical result is that

H^{i}_{\dR}(X/S)=\wedge^{i}H^{1}_{\dR}(X/S),

H^{1}_{\dR}(X/S)
is locally free of rank
2n
(and thus the
H^{i}_{\dR}(X/S)
are also finite locally free), the
R^{j}f_{*}{}_{X/S}^{j}
are finite locally free and the Hodge–de Rham spectral sequence degenerates (see
[
BBM82
, Proposition 2.5.2]
).

In this way, we can associate to any abelian scheme
X\rightarrow S
of relative dimension
n
and any
i\in\mathbb{N}
an
F
-zip
\underline{H}^{i}_{\dR}(X/S)
.
We can even go further and say that
\underline{H}^{i}_{\dR}(X/S)
is characterized by
H^{1}_{\dR}(X/S)
,
i.e.

\underline{H}^{i}_{\dR}(X/S)=\wedge^{i}\underline{H}^{1}_{\dR}(X/S)

(see
[
PWZ15
, Example 9.9]
).

Therefore, Lemma
4.5
(or rather its proof) implies the following.

Proposition 4.7
.

Let
f\colon X\rightarrow S
be an abelian scheme of relative dimension
n
. The derived
F
-zip
\underline{R\Gamma_{\dR}(X/S)}
is equivalent to the derived
F
-zip
\bigoplusop\displaylimits_{k=0}^{2n}\wedge^{k}\underline{H}^{1}_{\dR}(X/S)[k]

(
see Remark
4.4
for the notation
)
.

Proof.

See the discussion above.
∎

4.2.2. Proper smooth curves

Let
C
be a proper smooth connected curve of genus
g
over an algebraically closed field
k
of characteristic
p>0
. The de Rham complex consists of two terms. By the degeneracy of the spectral sequence, we know that

H^{n}_{\dR}(C/k)\cong\begin{cases}\Gamma(C,{\mathcal{O}}_{C})=k&\text{ if }n=0,\\
H^{1}(C,{\mathcal{O}}_{C})\oplus\Gamma(C,{}^{1}_{C/k})&\text{ if }n=1,\\
H^{1}(C,{}^{1}_{C/k})=k&\text{ if }n=2,\\
0&\text{ else}.\end{cases}

Further, we know that
g=\dim_{k}H^{1}(C,{\mathcal{O}}_{C})=\dim\Gamma(C,{}_{C/k}^{1})
. Therefore, the de Rham hypercohomology of
C
is a perfect complex of Tor-amplitude in
[-2,0]
, and the filtrations are of the form

\displaystyle\textup{{HDG}}^{\bullet}\colon\cdots\longrightarrow 0\longrightarrow\textup{{HDG}}^{1}\longrightarrow R\Gamma_{\dR}(C/k)\longrightarrow R\Gamma_{\dR}(C/k)\longrightarrow\cdots

\displaystyle\textup{{conj}}_{\bullet}\colon\cdots\longrightarrow 0\longrightarrow\textup{{conj}}_{0}\longrightarrow R\Gamma_{\dR}(C/k)\longrightarrow R\Gamma_{\dR}(C/k)\longrightarrow\cdots.

The graded pieces are given by

\gr^{0}\textup{{HDG}}\simeq R\Gamma(C,{\mathcal{O}}_{C})\quad\textup{and}\quad\gr^{1}\textup{{HDG}}\simeq R\Gamma(C,{}^{1}_{C/k})[-1],

which are perfect complexes of Tor-amplitude in
[-1,0]
and
[-2,-1]
(in homological notation).

Let
{\mathcal{M}}
denote the moduli stack of smooth proper curves
X\rightarrow S
(see
[
Sta24
, 0DMJ]
). The map

R\Gamma_{\dR}\colon X/S\longmapsto\underline{R\Gamma_{\dR}(X/S)}

from
{\mathcal{M}}
to
t_{0}\FZip^{[-2,0],\{-1,0\}}
gives a decomposition
{\mathcal{M}}=\coprodop\displaylimits_{g}{\mathcal{M}}_{g}
into open and closed substacks classifying smooth proper curves of genus
g
. This follows from the fact that the Euler characteristic of the graded pieces of the de Rham hypercohomology are determined by the genus and from Proposition
3.43
.

Consider the function
\sigma\colon\mathbb{Z}\rightarrow\mathbb{N}_{0}{Z}
defined as
\sigma(0)_{0}=\sigma(1)_{-2}=1
and
\sigma(0)_{-1}=\sigma(1)_{-1}=g
for some
g\in\mathbb{N}
and with value zero otherwise. An example of a strong derived
F
-zip homotopy finite projective of type
\sigma
is
\underline{R\Gamma_{\dR}(C/k)}
by the above (the strongness follows from Theorem
3.74
). As the de Rham cohomologies are finite projective and the Hodge spectral sequence degenerates, we see that locally
\underline{R\Gamma_{\dR}(C/k)}
is determined by the graded pieces of the Hodge and conjugate filtrations (see Lemma
3.69
). This allows us to construct
